\Cref{fig:hyper-gadget} shows one hyperedge in isolation. \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget-example} carries the idea to a whole hypergraph in two panels. Panel (a) is the hypergraph drawn as a metro map: each of the eight hyperedges is one coloured line threading its three member stations, vertex to vertex, and a station on several hyperedges shows several coloured lines running through it. Panel (b) reads the same map as the flow network. For one pair of stations, $u$ and $v$, it adds the helper point of every hyperedge a route boards, drawn as a small gate $h_{\bullet}$ in that line's colour, and traces the edge-disjoint Berge routes between $u$ and $v$ as thin black lines passing through those gates, with every hyperedge rail faded to the background. The checker reads $\lec(u, v) = 4$: two routes run straight through the two hyperedges that contain both $u$ and $v$, and two longer ones change lines at an intermediate station, four in all, while a fifth would have to reuse a gate.

One way to picture the helper point is the metro map of \Cref{ch:basecases}: each hyperedge is a metro line, its member vertices are the stations on it, and the helper is the line itself. Boarding at any station counts as taking that line. Because only one route may pass through the helper, only one train runs on the line at a time, so a second route must reach its destination without ever boarding it. The same flow computation that counts edge-disjoint paths through ordinary links now counts routes that share no line, which is exactly what Berge edge-disjointness means.

On the complete graph $K_n$ the hypergraph checker returns $n - 1$, and on the complete three-uniform hypergraph $K_4^{(3)}$ it returns $3$, larger than the two hyperedges through each pair because longer Berge routes also carry flow. The self-test confirms both of these values, so the construction is checked rather than merely asserted. The same helper-point network also answers the vertex-disjoint hypergraph question without alteration: splitting each ordinary vertex as in \Cref{fig:vertex-split} counts internally vertex-disjoint Berge routes. The one remaining direction, the \emph{directed} hypergraph, needs the helper point to learn which way is which, and that is the subject of the next subsection.

\subsection{The directed hypergraph checker}
A \emph{directed} hyperedge carries an orientation: it is a single \emph{tail} vertex together with $r-1$ \emph{head} vertices, drawn as the star of \Cref{fig:dir-hyper-convention}, and a directed Berge route may only enter it at the tail and leave it at one of its heads. The undirected helper point treated a hyperedge as a line any route could board at any station. The directed one must instead be a one-way turnstile: a route may step onto the hyperedge only at its tail and step off only at a head. The gadget that enforces this is the helper point made directional. For a hyperedge $e = (a;\, b, c, \dots)$ we add a gate node $g_e$, send one capacity-one arc from the tail into it, $a \to g_e$, and send an arc out of it to each head, $g_e \to b$, $g_e \to c$, and so on (\Cref{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}). A route reaches $g_e$ only through the tail $a$, and the single unit of capacity on $a \to g_e$ lets just one route use the hyperedge, exactly as the undirected turnstile did, but now it can leave only towards a head. The gate is built once per hyperedge, directed or not, by the same loop:

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The gate loop behind \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget} and \Cref{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}: one capacity-one gate per hyperedge, wired one-way for a directed hyperedge and both ways for an undirected one.}, label={offcut:lst:gate}]
gate_in, gate_out = base + 2 * index, base + 2 * index + 1
cap[gate_in, gate_out] = 1           # one route through this hyperedge
if hypergraph.directed:
    tails, heads = _dir_tails_heads(edge)
    for tail in tails:
        cap[leave(tail), gate_in] = _UNBOUNDED
    for head in heads:
        cap[gate_out, enter(head)] = _UNBOUNDED
\end{lstlisting}

\centering

\caption{The directed helper point. A directed hyperedge $e = (a;\, b, c)$, the tail $a$ fanning to its heads $b, c$ (left), becomes a gate node $g_e$ fed by one capacity-one arc from the tail and feeding an arc to each head (right). A route may reach $g_e$ only through the tail and may leave only towards a head, so the gate is a one-way version of the turnstile of \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget}, and the single unit of capacity still lets only one route use the hyperedge.}
\offcutlabel{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}
\end{figure}

the directed whole-hypergraph example reads the same hypergraph directed, again in two panels. Panel (a) gives the whole hypergraph oriented, every hyperedge a one-way line whose arrows point away from its tail, so the middle-tailed lines fan out from their centre and the end-tailed ones run one way along their length. Panel (b) traces the routes from $v$ to $u$, the gate rule of \Cref{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}, fading every hyperedge rail to the background and threading a black arrow from the route's entry station through the one-route gate $h_{\bullet}$, set in a clear gap off the rails, to its exit, with a dotted leg to the hyperedge's third member. Orientation costs connectivity. Of the four undirected routes between $u$ and $v$ only two survive the arrows, $\lec(v, u) = 2$, because $v$ can set out only through the two hyperedges of which it is the tail, and the two survivors cross at the interchange $l$ so they share no hyperedge. The same vertex-splitting trick of \Cref{fig:vertex-split} laid on top then counts internally vertex-disjoint directed routes, so this one construction measures all four hypergraph variants and \code{solve} reaches every one of the twelve problems through a single checker.

\caption[Directed Berge routes on a whole hypergraph]{The hypergraph
of \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget-example} read directed. Panel (a) gives the whole directed
hypergraph, every hyperedge a one-way line whose arrows all run \emph{away} from
its tail station, so a route may join a line at its tail and ride it to a head.
Because the map keeps each hyperedge on the track it already occupied, a line
whose tail is an end station is oriented along its own length rather than fanned
out as the star of \Cref{fig:dir-hyper-convention}, so both renderings appear here.
One rule reads either of them off the picture: the tail of a coloured line is the
single station of that line into which none of its arrows points, and the other
$r-1$ stations are its heads. Applied here it gives the eight tail-and-heads
splits $a \to ed$, $m \to nj$, $c \to le$, $d \to em$, $l \to en$, $m \to le$,
$j \to cm$ and $l \to dj$. Panel
(b) traces the routes from $v$ to $u$. Every hyperedge rail is faded to the
background, and a black arrow runs from the route's entry station through the
hyperedge's one-route gate $h_{\bullet}$ to its exit, the directed turnstile of
\Cref{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}, with the gate set in a clear gap off the rails. A dotted
leg in the line's colour ties each gate to the hyperedge's third member, the station
the route does not visit. Two edge-disjoint directed Berge routes from $v$ to $u$
survive. The long route
$v \to j \to c \to l \to u$ rides \textcolor{metroB}{nmj}, \textcolor{metroG}{mcj},
\textcolor{metroC}{elc} and \textcolor{metroF}{nel} in turn, and the short route
$v \to l \to d \to u$ rides \textcolor{metroH}{elm}, \textcolor{metroE}{ldj} and
\textcolor{metroD}{edm}. They cross only at the interchange $l$, so they share no
hyperedge, and the checker reads $\lec(v, u) = 2$, since $v$ can set out only through
the two hyperedges of which it is the tail. Against the undirected four of
\Cref{fig:hyper-gadget-example}, the arrows have halved the count.}
\offcutlabel{fig:dir-hyper-gadget-example}
\end{figure}

The gate is indifferent to how a hyperedge's vertices are split between tails and heads. It draws one capacity-one arc in from every tail and one out to every head, so the single forward tail it was built for is only the simplest case: give it a hyperedge with several tails and one head, or several of each, and it still counts the routes that enter at a tail and leave at a head. The same checker therefore measures every \emph{orientation model} of a directed Berge edge, not just the one-tail one, and \Cref{sec:orientation-models} takes up which of those models are worth distinguishing and what the program finds for them.

With the checker in place the proposed value of \Cref{prop:hyper-edge} can be tested on any small hypergraph the way every other variant is, which is the subject of the next section.
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{E4. The essay-form original-contributions list}{E4}{main.tex, former Contribution Statement list}{after the contribution statement opening paragraphs}{The live table keeps the same five contribution groups, variant scope, and result status, followed by the unchanged authorship and AI-credit disclosures.}
What is new in this thesis is told in full in \Cref{ch:synthesis} and proved in \Cref{app:proofs}. Listed compactly, and grouped by which of the twelve variants each concerns, my own results are these.

\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Hyperedge networks, vertex separation.} The problem is settled completely at $m = 2$, for every hyperedge size $r$ and every $n$ (\Cref{thm:hyper-vertex-m2}). At $m = 3$ I prove the universal upper bound $\floor{(m-1)(n-1)/(r-1)}$ for $r$-uniform multihypergraphs and prove it attained whenever $r \ge 3$ and $2 \le \binom{n-2}{r-2}$ (\Cref{thm:hyper-vertex-m3}), so in that range requiring routes to avoid one another's interiors costs nothing. The upper bound is the harder argument and rests on a new rank bound for incidence graphs, proved by way of Tutte's decomposition into three-connected pieces (\Cref{lem:incidence-rank}).

  \item \emph{One-way multigraphs.} The exact value, for every $n$ and every $m$ (\Cref{thm:dir-multi-full}), closing what had stood as a conjecture confirmed only to six vertices. The idea is to delete a whole sparse spanning subgraph chosen to preserve reachability, rather than one vertex at a time, which avoids the obstruction that stopped the point-by-point argument at odd $n$ and needs no restriction on $m$. In three lines it also reproves by hand the finite fact $L_3^{\mathrm{dir}}(7) = 24$, to which the earlier route had reduced the whole $m = 3$ problem. Read backwards from equality the same argument is rigid, and classifies the extremal digraphs on the quadratic branch whenever $n \ge \max(8, 2m)$ as the balanced one-way complete bipartite digraphs at multiplicity $m-1$ (\Cref{thm:dir-multi-uniqueness}).

  \item \emph{One-way simple graphs, arc separation.} The exact value is open for $m \ge 3$; what I prove is an unconditional bound on it, $\floor{n^2/4} + 4(m-1)(n-1)$ arcs under any route limit $m$ (\Cref{prop:dir-arc-stability,thm:dir-arc-linear-error}). Together these fix the leading constant of the standing conjecture (\Cref{conj:dir-arc}) and the order of its second term with no assumption about the shape of the extremiser, which is exactly what every previous route to that bound had needed. Separately, and this is a claim about shape rather than size, deleting at most $\sqrt{m}\,n^{3/2}$ arcs makes any feasible digraph a subgraph of a one-way bipartite graph. An external AI review suggested the counting idea behind this estimate. I verified the argument and derived the sharpening stated here.

  \item \emph{Multigraphs, vertex separation, counting parallel copies as distinct routes.} This is the least understood of the twelve. I settle it at $m = 2$, then disprove the natural guess: the thickened tree the data up to $m = 4$ suggest is beaten, for every $m \ge 5$, by a bouquet of thickened cliques sharing one point, by a margin growing linearly in $n$ (\Cref{thm:clique-chain-vertex}), and a matching upper bound gives the right joint order in the regime $n/m \to \infty$ (\Cref{prop:multi-vertex-upper}). Even the bouquet is not the answer: the value is exactly a \emph{block} problem, a knapsack over the best two-connected block of each size (\Cref{thm:multi-vertex-blocks}), which settles it for every $n \le 8$ and $m \le 8$ and finds dense, bipartite-like winning blocks; the complete-bipartite family supplies the improved asymptotic construction (\Cref{thm:multi-vertex-bipartite}).

  \item \emph{Two results from isolating what the quadratic bound uses.} The answer is very little: one cap on one family of routes, and after that nothing but counting degrees (\Cref{lem:two-step-budget}), so any variant able to supply the cap inherits the bound. Hence $k_m^{\mathrm{dir}}(n) = n^2/4 + \Theta_m(n)$ for every fixed $m \ge 3$ (\Cref{thm:dir-vertex-linear-error}), a legitimate transfer because what carries across is the family of routes counted and not the bound itself, Whitney's inequality running the wrong way for the latter. And the leading constant for one-way hyperedge networks is settled outright at $(m-1)n^2/\bigl(4(r-1)\bigr)$, under both separations at once (\Cref{thm:dir-hyper-constant}), then extended to every way of splitting a hyperedge into tails and heads (\Cref{thm:dir-hyper-general-constant}), so the orientation axis closes to first order. These two reuse the externally suggested counting idea credited above, and my contribution here is the weaker hypothesis and the two arguments that supply it. The multigraph upper bound just mentioned is not one of them, and reaches its conclusion by an unrelated route through Mader's density theorem.
\end{itemize}
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{E5. The expanded software and reproducibility appendix}{E5}{chapters/app\_code.tex, former whole appendix}{the Software and Reproducibility appendix}{The live appendix retains the archive contents, environment command, evidence ladder, test and figure commands, code map, and archival checklist in compact tables.}
The software is part of the evidence behind the finite computations in this thesis, but printing every source file would make the archival document harder rather than easier to audit. Source code is most useful in its native, executable form: with its directory structure intact, under version control, and accompanied by tests and machine-readable data. This appendix therefore records the complete audit trail without duplicating roughly one hundred and forty pages of listings.

The authoritative source tree is maintained at
\begin{center}
\url{https://github.com/louisvandenbruwaene/thesis}.
\end{center}
For the final submission, an archived snapshot rather than a moving branch name should be treated as definitive. It should be identified by a Git tag or commit hash and stored together with the PDF. A reader can then verify the checked-out version with
\begin{verbatim}
git rev-parse HEAD
git status --short
\end{verbatim}
The first command identifies the exact revision, and the second should print nothing for an unmodified archive. This pairing matters: a commit hash alone does not reveal uncommitted changes.

\section{What is in the archive}\offcutlabel{app:software-layout}

The computational material is deliberately divided by responsibility:

\begin{description}[style=nextline]
  \item[\code{program/erdos915\_unified.py}]
  The mathematical model, exact connectivity routines, finite certifiers, exhaustive enumerators, heuristic searches, random samplers, and the public \code{solve} interface. It computes all numerical extremal results except the two block sweeps named immediately below.

  \item[\code{research\_notes/scripts/simple\_vertex\_blocks.py} and
        \code{research\_notes/scripts/multi\_vertex\_blocks.py}]
  The two exceptions to the preceding evidential path. They enumerate
  $2$-connected simple blocks with \code{geng}; the first supplies the sweep
  behind $c_m$ and the second generates \Cref{tab:multi-vertex-exact} and solves
  its knapsack. From the repository root the archived runs are
  \begin{verbatim}
  BMAX=9 .venv/bin/python3 research_notes/scripts/simple_vertex_blocks.py
  BMAX=8 .venv/bin/python3 research_notes/scripts/multi_vertex_blocks.py
  \end{verbatim}
  The second script's terminal output is retained as
  \path{figures/multi_vertex_blocks_log.txt}.

  \item[\code{program/tests/}]
  The independent regression suite. Its tests cover the graph and hypergraph representations, edge and vertex connectivity, known closed forms, named constructions, exhaustive enumeration, certification, search invariants, and random-model utilities.

  \item[\code{program/make\_figures.py}]
  The figure driver. It calls the routines in the main program and writes the plots in \code{figures/}. Randomised calls use fixed seeds.

  \item[\code{program/\_erdos\_fast.c} and \code{program/build\_fast.sh}]
  An optional accelerator for selected inner loops. The Python implementation remains the reference path, and tests compare both implementations when the compiled helper is present.

  \item[\code{program/requirements.txt}]
  The declared Python dependencies and supported version ranges.

  \item[Execution transcripts]
  The files \path{figures/certificate_log.txt}, \path{figures/basecase_search_log.txt}, \path{figures/basecase_search_vertex_log.txt}, and \path{figures/multi_vertex_blocks_log.txt} are plain-text records of finite runs used in the thesis. They record particular executions and are not substitutes for the source or for the mathematical interpretation of their status.

  \item[\code{figures/*.json}, \code{figures/*.pkl}, and generated images]
  Cached data and presentation outputs. The JSON files are human-readable, while the pickle files are Python caches and must never be accepted from an untrusted source. None is a hand proof.

  \item[\code{research\_notes/}]
  Exploratory work, conjectures, and supporting scripts. Apart from the block-table generator explicitly named above, a note in this directory is not part of the thesis merely because it exists in the repository. Only results stated in the thesis, with the proof or evidential status stated there, are claimed.
\end{description}

\section{Recreating the environment}\offcutlabel{app:software-environment}

The core program requires Python 3, NumPy, and SciPy. PuLP supplies the optional mixed-integer certifier, Matplotlib renders figures, and NetworkX supplies the optional Gomory--Hu comparison, and \code{geng} from \textsc{nauty} supports one generation pipeline. From the repository root, a clean virtual environment can be prepared with

\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pip install --upgrade pip
python -m pip install -r program/requirements.txt
\end{verbatim}

The requirements file records compatible ranges rather than freezing platform-specific binary wheels. For a long-term replication, the replicator should additionally record

\begin{verbatim}
python --version
python -m pip freeze
uname -a
\end{verbatim}

and retain that output with the run transcript. Solver-backed claims also require the solver name, version, termination status, objective value, best bound, and reported optimality gap. Merely reporting that a solver returned a number is insufficient.

The optional C helper can be built from \code{program/} with

\begin{verbatim}
bash build_fast.sh
\end{verbatim}
Its absence changes performance, not the mathematical definition or expected answer. A replication intended to test correctness rather than speed should first run without the helper and then, if desired, repeat with it enabled.

\section{The verification ladder}\offcutlabel{app:verification-ladder}

The program uses five logically different kinds of computation. Their outputs must not be conflated.

\begin{table}[ht]
  \centering
  \small
  \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.20\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
    \toprule
    Method & Legitimate conclusion & Required audit evidence \\
    \midrule
    Exact max flow & The connectivity of the supplied finite object & Input object, separation convention, exact-capacity construction, and agreement with invariant tests \\
    Exhaustive enumeration & A finite optimum, provided every candidate in the stated space is covered & Generation rule, scope, symmetry handling, pruning justification, and a completed run \\
    MILP certification & A finite upper bound when the model is sound and optimality is certified & Full formulation, solver status, objective and bound, zero gap, and independently checked witness for the lower bound \\
    Heuristic search & A concrete feasible construction and hence a lower bound & The witness itself and an exact connectivity check. Run time or failure to improve never proves optimality \\
    Monte Carlo sampling & An empirical distribution for the specified sampler and seed & Sampling rule, parameters, seed, and sample count, never an extremal proof or an asymptotic theorem \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabularx}
  \caption{What each computational method can and cannot establish.}
  \offcutlabel{tab:software-evidence}
\end{table}

This hierarchy is enforced at the interface: \code{solve} labels returned information as exact, an upper bound, or a lower bound according to the method used. The prose of the thesis follows the same rule. In particular, a search result becomes a certified optimum only after an independent upper-bound argument meets it.

\section{Running the checks}\offcutlabel{app:software-tests}

From \code{program/}, the two principal checks are

\begin{verbatim}
python erdos915_unified.py
python -m unittest discover -s tests -v
\end{verbatim}

The first runs the built-in invariant checks. The second runs the separate unit-test suite. Optional-dependency tests report \code{skipped} when their dependency is unavailable, and a green run with skipped tests therefore certifies only the exercised subset. A replication report should preserve the complete output and distinguish passed, failed, errored, and skipped tests.

The most important cross-checks are structural rather than numerical. They include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item edge connectivity on trees, cycles, complete graphs, and parallel-edge examples whose values are known by inspection
  \item vertex connectivity against Whitney's inequality and against an independently constructed split-vertex flow network
  \item hypergraph connectivity reducing to ordinary graph connectivity at uniformity two
  \item fast predicates agreeing with the uncapped exact routines
  \item canonical forms remaining invariant under relabelling
  \item named constructions simultaneously meeting their claimed edge count and connectivity ceiling
  \item exhaustive small cases reproducing published or hand-proved values
  \item heuristic witnesses being rechecked by the exact connectivity routine before they are reported.
\end{itemize}

A test can reveal an inconsistency, but passing tests do not prove an untested general theorem. This is why every general theorem in the thesis has a mathematical proof or a literature citation independently of the program.

\section{Reproducing figures and finite outputs}\offcutlabel{app:software-reproduction}

All externally generated computational figures can be regenerated from \code{program/} with

\begin{verbatim}
python make_figures.py
\end{verbatim}
The script writes into \code{figures/}. Because the random seeds are fixed, repeating a run with the same source and compatible numerical libraries should reproduce the underlying data, with the single exception of the timing columns of \Cref{tab:sa-vs-tabu}, which are wall-clock and therefore representative rather than seed-exact. Raster or PDF bytes can nevertheless differ across Matplotlib, font, or compression versions even when the plotted numbers agree. Numerical data and labels should therefore be compared before file hashes.

The finite run logs are kept beside the figures: \code{figures/basecase\_search\_log.txt} and \code{figures/basecase\_search\_vertex\_log.txt} are the two exhaustive base-case runs tabulated in \Cref{sec:transcripts}, and \code{figures/certificate\_log.txt} is the historical cut-counting solver record summarised there.

The finite certificates deserve a stricter protocol:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item check out the archived revision and confirm a clean worktree
  \item record the software and solver versions
  \item run the complete test suite and record all skips
  \item run the relevant certifier with the same parameters as the transcript
  \item require a completed optimal solve with zero reported gap
  \item extract an extremal witness and check its edge count and every relevant local connectivity independently
  \item compare the objective, bound, and witness with the statement in the thesis.
\end{enumerate}

For long exhaustive runs, intermediate checkpoints may establish that work was performed but do not establish completion. Only the terminal output, together with the coverage argument for the generator and pruning rules, supports the finite upper bound.

\section{Mapping the thesis to the code}\offcutlabel{app:software-map}

Names rather than printed line numbers are the stable references, since line numbers change whenever documentation or tests are improved. The principal entry points are:

\begin{description}[style=nextline]
  \item[Graph and hypergraph objects]
  \code{Graph}, \code{Hypergraph}, and \code{Variant} define the finite objects and the direction, simplicity, multiplicity, uniformity, and separation choices.

  \item[Exact measurement]
  \code{max\_edge\_connectivity}, \code{max\_vertex\_connectivity}, \code{max\_hyperedge\_connectivity}, and \code{max\_hyper\_vertex\_connectivity} implement the quantities defined in \Cref{ch:basecases}. Each is one pass of \code{local\_edge\_connectivity}, a single \code{scipy} integer maximum flow whose arc capacities are the multiplicities $\mu$, over every pair, ordered if the variant is directed, returning the largest value. The vertex routines run the same flow on the split capacity matrix \code{\_split\_capacity\_matrix(G)}, in which every adjacency has capacity one, so parallel edges never raise $\kappa$. The hypergraph routines run it on \code{\_hyper\_capacity\_matrix(H)}, in which each hyperedge becomes a helper point only one route may pass through, and agree with ordinary edge-connectivity on two-vertex hyperedges. \code{max\_edge\_connectivity\_via\_tree} is an undirected-only shortcut that reads $\lambda^{\max}$ off the heaviest edge of one Gomory--Hu tree, $n-1$ flows in place of $\binom{n}{2}$, and doubles as a cross-check on the pairwise sweep.

  \item[Search instrumentation]
  \code{edge\_sensitivity} returns $\sigma(e) = \lambda^{\max}(G) - \lambda^{\max}(G-e)$ by deleting $e$ and remeasuring, and \code{sensitivity\_map} computes it for the whole graph from one shared baseline. This is the quantity the cooling schedule of \Cref{ch:discover} biases its removals by.

  \item[Certification and enumeration]
  The functions documented under the certifier and exhaustive-enumeration sections of \code{program/README.md} support \Cref{ch:certify} and the finite transcripts in \Cref{sec:transcripts}.

  \item[Discovery]
  The simulated-annealing engine is \path{search_for_dense_graph}, and the tabu engine is \path{tabu_search_for_dense_graph}. Both implement the searches described in \Cref{ch:discover}, and their results are lower bounds until separately certified.

  \item[Unified entry point]
  \code{solve} selects the applicable exact, certifying, or searching method and returns the evidential status with the result.

  \item[Presentation]
  \code{make\_figures.py} and the plotting functions generate the images, while the mathematical data originate in the same model and measurement routines used elsewhere.
\end{description}

The repository's \code{program/README.md} gives the current function-level map and command examples. If it ever conflicts with the archived source or with the mathematical definitions in the thesis, the source determines what was executed and the thesis determines what is claimed. The discrepancy must then be reported rather than silently reconciled.

\section{Archival checklist}\offcutlabel{app:software-archive}

For the submitted artefact to remain independently auditable, the following items should be deposited together:

\begin{itemize}
  \item the final PDF
  \item the complete clean source tree, including tests and figure-generation scripts
  \item an immutable Git tag or commit identifier for that tree
  \item the dependency specification and, preferably, a frozen environment listing
  \item the solver names and versions used for finite certificates
  \item the plain-text terminal transcripts on which finite claims rely
  \item the concrete extremal witnesses in a machine-readable format
  \item a cryptographic checksum manifest for the deposited files.
\end{itemize}

The working file \code{offcuts.tex}, which records the material removed from
this document during its final shortening pass, is deliberately \emph{not} part
of the submitted artefact. It is never \code{\textbackslash input} into
\code{main.tex} and builds to a separate PDF. It is kept in the repository
because it renders several figures that \code{main.tex} no longer references,
so no file in \code{figures/} may be removed on the grounds of being unused.

One portable way to create the last item on macOS is

\begin{verbatim}
find . -type f ! -path './.git/*' ! -name 'SHA256SUMS' -print0 |
  sort -z | xargs -0 shasum -a 256 > SHA256SUMS
\end{verbatim}
The manifest should be created only after the archive is final and should itself be stored alongside, not recursively included in, the hashed set. Together, the immutable revision, clean-worktree check, environment record, tests, transcripts, witnesses, and checksums provide stronger reproducibility than a static code listing inside the PDF.
\end{offcut}

\section*{Group F: merged machine-method chapter}

The live source is consolidated in chapters/ch2\_machine.tex.}

\nowreads

\label{ch:machine}\label{ch:certify}\label{ch:discover}

\end{verbatim}
Both former labels remain aliases on the combined chapter, so old restore snippets and external references continue to resolve.
\end{offcutrewrite}

\begin{offcutrewrite}{F2. The separate certification-chapter opening and pipeline framing}{F2}{former chapters/ch2\_certify.tex, opening}{immediately after the combined Chapter 2 declaration}{The merged opening states the same prove/discover evidential distinction once for the whole pipeline.}
The last chapter ended in three different kinds of stuckness, and all three share a shape. The objects are graphs of one flavour or another, the constraint is always a ceiling on connectivity, and the work is always either measuring a given graph or checking many small ones. That shared shape is what a single program can exploit. This chapter builds it, and then uses it for one purpose only: to \emph{prove}. It gives every variant one representation, measures connectivity exactly through a max-flow checker, checks small cases by honest enumeration, and turns the finite checks the proofs of \Cref{ch:basecases} relied on into a single auditable certifier. The hunt for dense examples, which proves nothing on its own, waits until \Cref{ch:discover}.

A word on what the program is for. It is a microscope, not a source of final answers. It measures connectivity exactly, so what it reports about a given graph is a fact. It enumerates graphs of a fixed size, so for that size it can prove a theorem. It will later discover dense graphs, which are only lower bounds, but not here. Each of these has a different epistemic weight, and the program is built so that the three never get confused. \Cref{fig:spine} draws this spine: a single \emph{model} holds every variant, one exact \emph{measure} reads connectivity off it, and from that measurement a \emph{prove} step bounds a fixed size from above, with the \emph{discover} step of the next chapter and a final \emph{observe} step for the random model added later. Every routine introduced from here on is shown as a small card carrying its name, its inputs and output, a one-line account of how it works, and a coloured badge naming its place in the spine. The function bodies are not reproduced, because the point is the interface, not the code.

\noindent\emph{Former pipeline caption:}
The architecture as one pipeline. A single data model, the multiplicity matrix $\mu$, feeds an exact measurement, the max-flow checker. That measurement feeds the two bound-producing roles: a prover for upper bounds (the cut-counting optimisation and the exhaustive search), built in this chapter, and a discoverer for lower bounds (simulated annealing, or tabu search), built in \Cref{ch:discover}. A final sampling role studies the random model. The badge colours on the code cards below match the boxes here.

\noindent\emph{Former driver-card method sentence:}
picks the method the case allows: brute-force enumeration, the pruned search, or the cut-counting certifier when proving, or the temperature search or tabu search when discovering (\Cref{ch:discover}). It always respects the budget, and it is the one function the rest of the thesis ever calls.
\nowreads
The last chapter ended with several variants stuck for different reasons, but all ask one machine to do the same three jobs. It must represent every model, measure connectivity exactly, and then use that measurement either to \emph{prove} a finite optimum by exhaustive certification or to \emph{discover} a dense feasible graph. This chapter builds that single pipeline in that order. The epistemic distinction matters more than the shared implementation: measurement is exact, completed enumeration proves, and heuristic search supplies only a lower bound. \Cref{fig:spine} is the map. The small cards below name each routine and its interface; the function bodies stay in the archive because the argument needs the contract, not the code listing.

\noindent\emph{Current pipeline caption:}
The architecture as one pipeline. A single model feeds the exact max-flow checker; certification turns that measurement into finite upper bounds, while simulated annealing or tabu search uses it to discover lower bounds. The badge colours on the code cards match the boxes here.

\noindent\emph{Current driver-card method sentence:}
picks the method the case allows: brute-force enumeration, the pruned search, or the cut-counting certifier when proving, and the temperature search or tabu search when discovering. It always respects the budget, and it is the one function the rest of the thesis ever calls.

\begin{offcutrewrite}{F3. The separate certify-to-discover chapter transition}{F3}{former chapters/ch2\_certify.tex, end of the certification chapter}{before Reproducibility}{The new wording keeps the honesty boundary but no longer announces a chapter break that does not exist.}
\section{What the solver may claim}

Now that the pieces are built, it is worth stating plainly what each may claim. The checker \emph{measures}: its connectivity values are exact. Exhaustive enumeration and the pruned search \emph{prove} a finite optimum when they complete. The cut-counting solver supplies an independent finite check, but its status is not a replayable certificate. The search of \Cref{ch:discover} \emph{discovers}: it produces concrete graphs, hence lower bounds, and never an upper bound. For the directed multigraph row, \code{solve} labels the value exact because it invokes the hand theorem, not because it trusts a solver status.

With the microscope built and aimed at proof, the variants with small answers are settled and the directed base cases the hand proof needed are in hand. What proof by enumeration cannot do is reach the sizes where the answers are only conjectured, because the number of graphs explodes long before the interesting cases arrive. The next chapter measures that explosion exactly, and then turns the same program loose to \emph{discover} the dense graphs that blind enumeration can never reach.
\nowreads

\begin{offcutrewrite}{F4. The separate search-chapter opening and expanded enumeration-wall transition}{F4}{former chapters/ch3\_discover.tex, opening}{after the certification-standard subsection in the combined chapter}{The live transition introduces search as the second half of the same pipeline and removes repeated statements about why exhaustive enumeration stops.}
The previous chapter proved what could be proved by looking at every graph of a fixed size. That method is honest and exact, and it stops almost at once. This chapter begins by measuring exactly how fast it stops, because the speed of that collapse is the whole reason a second kind of tool is needed. Then it builds that tool. Where the certifier walks all graphs, the search walks a few of them well, cooling like a physical system toward the densest feasible shape. What it returns is never a proof. It is a concrete graph, hence a lower bound and a conjecture, a stepping stone toward an upper bound that a later proof or certificate must supply. The chapter ends by asking the question any such tool must face: how good is it, and how far from the truth might its answers be.

\section{The wall: how enumeration explodes}

The brute force of \Cref{ch:certify} visits every graph of a given size. The trouble is how many that is. A simple undirected graph on $n$ vertices is a yes-or-no choice on each of the $\binom{n}{2}$ possible edges, so there are $2^{\binom{n}{2}}$ of them. A digraph chooses on each of the $n(n-1)$ ordered pairs, giving $2^{n(n-1)}$, and allowing multiplicities up to a cap $c$ raises the base from two to $c + 1$. These are not numbers that merely grow. They detonate. \Cref{fig:complexity} draws the growth on a logarithmic scale, where each step up the axis multiplies the count tenfold, and the message is the same in every panel: the count passes any reachable budget while $n$ is still in the single digits.

Three readings of the figure matter for what follows. Adding vertices is the sharpest cost: each new vertex multiplies the count by an exponential in $n$, so a single extra vertex can move a problem from seconds to centuries. Raising the connectivity budget is the next cost, lifting the base of the exponent rather than its height, which is why the multigraph curves sit above the simple one in every panel. Direction is the third: a digraph has twice as many cells to fill as the undirected graph on the same vertices (and a directed $3$-uniform hyperedge, a tail with two heads, has three times as many), so the directed panel climbs fastest of all, and those are precisely the variants whose answers \Cref{ch:basecases} could not prove. The separation, by contrast, costs nothing here, because edge and vertex disjointness search the same graphs and differ only in what the checker measures, which is why a single pair of panels suffices for all of them. The certifier of \Cref{ch:certify} reaches a few vertices further than blind enumeration, because its cut-counting optimisation is polynomial in the size of the encoding rather than in the number of graphs, but solving that encoding is itself hard and the gain is measured in single vertices, not orders of magnitude. Past that, no exhaustive method reaches the interesting sizes. Something that does not look at every graph is required.

\noindent\emph{Former rediscovery-caption phrase:} ``proved or certified in the earlier chapters.''

\noindent\emph{Former honesty sentence:} ``The figures in this chapter use fixed random seeds, so every search point is reproducible.''
\nowreads
Certification is exact only while it can cover every candidate. The second half of this chapter measures where that ends and replaces the exhaustive walk by a selective one: simulated annealing or tabu search explores a few graphs well, using the same checker to keep the best feasible construction. Its output is a concrete graph and therefore a lower bound, never an upper bound.

\section{From certification to discovery: the enumeration wall}\offcutlabel{sec:search-wall}

The exhaustive methods above visit every graph of a given size. The trouble is how many that is. A simple undirected graph on $n$ vertices is a yes-or-no choice on each of the $\binom{n}{2}$ possible edges, so there are $2^{\binom{n}{2}}$ of them. A digraph chooses on each of the $n(n-1)$ ordered pairs, giving $2^{n(n-1)}$, and allowing multiplicities up to a cap $c$ raises the base from two to $c + 1$. These are not numbers that merely grow. They detonate. \Cref{fig:complexity} draws the growth on a logarithmic scale, where each step up the axis multiplies the count tenfold, and the message is the same in every panel: the count passes any reachable budget while $n$ is still in the single digits.

Three readings of the figure matter for what follows. Adding vertices is the sharpest cost: each new vertex multiplies the count by an exponential in $n$, so a single extra vertex can move a problem from seconds to centuries. Raising the connectivity budget is the next cost, lifting the base of the exponent rather than its height, which is why the multigraph curves sit above the simple one in every panel. Direction is the third: a digraph has twice as many cells to fill as the undirected graph on the same vertices (and a directed $3$-uniform hyperedge, a tail with two heads, has three times as many), so the directed panel climbs fastest of all, and those are precisely the variants whose answers \Cref{ch:basecases} could not prove. The separation, by contrast, costs nothing here, because edge and vertex disjointness search the same graphs and differ only in what the checker measures. The cut-counting certifier reaches a few vertices further than blind enumeration, but solving its encoding is itself hard and the gain is measured in single vertices, not orders of magnitude. Past that, no exhaustive method reaches the interesting sizes.

\begin{offcutrewrite}{G1. Superseded MILP tutorial and solver history}{G1}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Section 2.7}{the heading ``Proving every $m$ at once''}{The general hand theorem now settles every $n,m$. The live section keeps the independent formulation, both diagrams, the complete MILP, and the finite result, but removes textbook-level expansion and historical repetition.}
\noindent\emph{Former scaling explanation.}

The key is that $m$ can be scaled out of the problem completely, so that it never appears in the computation at all. Start with any directed multigraph that is feasible at level $m$, so $\lambda^{\max} \le m - 1$, and divide every multiplicity by $m - 1$. Two things happen at once. The multiplicities become weights $w(u, v)$ between $0$ and $1$, and since dividing every capacity by $m - 1$ divides every flow by the same factor, the maximum flow between each pair drops to at most one. The arc count, meanwhile, turns into the total weight $\sum_{u \ne v} w(u, v) = |A| / (m - 1)$. So a dense feasible multigraph becomes a heavy feasible weight matrix, and the other way round. \Cref{fig:scaling-reduction} carries one small multigraph through the division.

That inequality holds always, and it is the direction that does the proving. The reverse does not come for free, and it is worth being exact about why, because the two are easy to run together. Scaling \emph{up} needs an optimal weight matrix whose entries are whole multiples of $1/(m-1)$, and a real optimum has no reason to oblige. What closes the gap is not an argument but an observation about the answer the solve returns: the heaviest matrix it finds is the \emph{double star}, the bidirected star carrying weight one on both arcs $h \to v$ and $v \to h$ between the hub and every other vertex. Its weights are all $0$ or $1$, so multiplying them by $m - 1$ gives an honest integer multigraph, not a fractional one, with $2(n-1)(m-1)$ arcs and $\lambda^{\max} = m - 1$, for every $m$ at once.

Whenever the optimum is attained by such a $\{0,1\}$ matrix, the two bounds meet, so that one solve settles infinitely many values. This is exactly what happens at every $n \le 6$, which is what \Cref{thm:dir-multi-small} rests on. At the time these solves were run it was a verified property of those particular optima and not a theorem about all $n$, which is why \code{prove\_integral\_arc\_bound} exists at all: it answers an integral question, such as $L_3^{\mathrm{dir}}(7) = 24$, directly, without leaning on an unverified property of the fractional optimum. That property is now a theorem for every $n$ (\Cref{cor:mstar-integral}), so the identity above holds unconditionally, but the route to it runs the other way round from what this section expected. It is not that a closer look at the fractional problem settled the integral one. It is that the integral problem was settled outright, at every $m$ at once, and the fractional statement then followed by scaling a rational optimum up until its entries become whole. \Cref{thm:dir-multi-full} settles the integral question for every $n$ by an entirely different, elementary argument; the $n = 7$ MILP call was abandoned at its time limit and never rerun on a stronger solver.

\noindent\emph{Former scaling-figure caption.}

The scaling step for $m = 3$. Dividing each multiplicity by $m - 1 = 2$ maps the integer multigraph to a weight matrix with all values in $[0, 1]$ and max-flow at most one between every pair. The double arc $A \to B$ becomes weight $1$, the single arc $A \to C$ becomes weight $\tfrac{1}{2}$, and the absent arc $B \to C$ stays absent. Crucially, $m$ drops out, so a single optimisation bounds $L_m^{\mathrm{dir}}(n) \le (m-1)M^*(n)$ for every $m$ simultaneously. The reverse inequality needs a $\{0,1\}$ optimum, verified at $n\le6$; the hand proof supplies it unconditionally by another route.

\noindent\emph{Former cut and MILP tutorial.}

What remains is to compute $M^{*}(n)$, and the difficulty is that the constraint ``maximum flow at most one'' is itself the answer to a flow problem rather than a plain inequality on $w$. The max-flow/min-cut theorem removes it. The maximum $s$--$t$ flow equals the smallest capacity of any cut separating $s$ from $t$. A cut splits the vertices into a source side holding $s$ and a sink side holding $t$, and its capacity is the total weight of the arcs that cross from the source side to the sink side. Thus $\mathrm{maxflow}(s,t)\le1$ exactly when some such cut has capacity at most one. For every ordered pair $(s,t)$, the optimisation picks one cut and requires its capacity to stay at or below one.

A cut is described by a yes/no label $x^{st}_u$ on each vertex, equal to one on the source side, with $s$ fixed there and $t$ on the sink side. An arc $u\to v$ crosses exactly when $u$ is on the source side and $v$ is not, and a helper $p^{st}_{uv}$ then picks up its weight. Forcing the crossing weights to sum to at most one for every pair gives the exact feasible region, not a relaxation. If a solver exports both a primal value and an independently checkable matching bound, their equality certifies $M^*(n)$. The historical wrapper retained only \texttt{OPTIMAL} and a primal witness, so its transcript is a finite solver check rather than a replayable certificate.

The optimisation is a \emph{Mixed-Integer Linear Program}. ``Linear'' means every constraint and the objective are linear; ``mixed-integer'' means that continuous arc weights coexist with binary cut assignments. Allowing the cut labels to be fractional would describe a weaker condition, so keeping them in $\{0,1\}$ is essential.

Three quantities appear:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $w(u,v)\in[0,1]$ is the scaled multiplicity of $u\to v$;
  \item $x^{st}_u\in\{0,1\}$ is vertex $u$'s side in the cut chosen for $(s,t)$, with $x^{st}_s=1$ and $x^{st}_t=0$;
  \item $p^{st}_{uv}\ge0$ is the crossing weight, equal to $w(u,v)$ when $u\to v$ crosses source-to-sink and zero otherwise.
\end{itemize}

The first MILP constraint is an indicator. Its right-hand side $w(u,v)+x^{st}_u-x^{st}_v-1$ is positive only in the crossing case, where it equals $w(u,v)$. The former case table was:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{cclcc}
\toprule
$x^{st}_u$ & $x^{st}_v$ & meaning & right-hand side & forces \\
\midrule
$1$ & $0$ & arc crosses source-to-sink & $w(u,v)$ & $p^{st}_{uv}\ge w(u,v)$ \\
$1$ & $1$ & both source side & $w(u,v)-1\le0$ & $p^{st}_{uv}\ge0$ \\
$0$ & $0$ & both sink side & $w(u,v)-1\le0$ & $p^{st}_{uv}\ge0$ \\
$0$ & $1$ & arc crosses backwards & $w(u,v)-2\le0$ & $p^{st}_{uv}\ge0$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The four cases of the crossing-weight constraint. Restore with the original label \code{tab:crossing-cases}.}
\end{table}

The second constraint caps the total crossing weight at one. By max-flow/min-cut the two lines together say that every ordered pair admits a cut of capacity at most one. What the archived output lacks is an independently replayable record that the solver reached its optimum.

The last two lines change no answer. The direct arc $s\to t$, together with one two-step detour $s\to x\to t$ through each intermediate vertex, already forms that many routes, so
\[
w(s,t)+\sum_x\min\bigl(w(s,x),w(x,t)\bigr)\le1
\]
holds for every feasible matrix and merely tightens the relaxation. The minimum is linearised with a continuous helper $z^{st}_x$ and binary selector $b^{st}_x$: two upper bounds keep $z$ below both arguments, and the lower bounds
\[
z^{st}_x\ge w(s,x)-(1-b^{st}_x),\qquad
z^{st}_x\ge w(x,t)-b^{st}_x
\]
force it to the selected minimum. The degree-ordering line removes relabelled copies without changing the optimum.

\noindent\emph{Former cut-figure caption.}

One ordered pair's chosen cut. The dashed line splits the vertices into the source side, where $x^{st}_u=1$, and the sink side, where $x^{st}_u=0$. Only arcs running from source to sink cross the cut, and the first constraint forces their helper $p^{st}_{uv}$ up to $w$ (the two thick arcs). Same-side and backward arcs contribute nothing; the second constraint caps the thick arcs at one.

\noindent\emph{Former solver-history close.}

It is worth being clear about what the historical phrase ``for all $m$'' meant. The optimisation tested a single $m$-free number $M^*(n)$. Because its optimum was attained by a $\{0,1\}$ matrix, scaling carried that finite solver result to every $m$ at once. The hand proof now establishes the same conclusion for all $n,m$ without relying on those runs.

Historical runs reported $M^*(3)=4$, $M^*(4)=6$, $M^*(5)=8$, and $M^*(6)=10$, each with status \texttt{OPTIMAL}. The first three finished quickly and $n=6$ took about twenty minutes. The archived run retained no separately checkable best bound or gap; $n=7$ did not close. These are performance facts about one formulation, not dependencies of the theorem.
\nowreads
The live section states the scaling argument once, retains the complete displayed MILP and both diagrams, explains the crossing indicator in one paragraph, and records the $n\le6$ result and its evidential limitation without the generic MILP primer.
\end{offcutrewrite}

\begin{offcutrewrite}{G2. Repeated evidence and reproducibility framing}{G2}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Sections 2.8, 2.9, 2.9.1, and 2.15}{the final results grid}{The same proved/certified/conjectured distinction appeared before and after the search, and the software appendix already carries the operational detail. The live chapter now states the standard once beside the results.}
\section{What the solver may claim}

Now that the pieces are built, it is worth stating plainly what each may claim. The checker \emph{measures}: its connectivity values are exact. Exhaustive enumeration and the pruned search \emph{prove} a finite optimum when they complete. The cut-counting solver supplies an independent finite check, but its status is not a replayable certificate. The search below \emph{discovers}: it produces concrete graphs, hence lower bounds, and never an upper bound. For the directed multigraph row, \code{solve} labels the value exact because it invokes the hand theorem, not because it trusts a solver status. Sharing one chapter does not merge these standards.

\section{Reproducibility}

Every claim in this thesis that rests on computation re-runs from a single Python driver. All of the program's logic lives in \code{program/erdos915\_unified.py}, one self-contained file whose core needs only \code{numpy} and \code{scipy}, because every connectivity value it reports is one \code{scipy} integer maximum flow on a capacity matrix. \code{pulp} sits on top for the certifier, and \code{matplotlib} and \code{networkx} only for presentation. \Cref{app:code} carries the rest in full: the archive layout, environment, entry points, commands, figure map, and archival protocol. The finite solver transcripts are reproduced in \Cref{sec:transcripts}.

\subsection{What a machine-checked claim in this thesis means}

Reproducibility is not the same as certification, and it is worth stating plainly what standard the computed results here meet, since a solver reporting \texttt{OPTIMAL} is not by itself a mathematical certificate. Three practices carry the weight. The exhaustive searches are self-certifying in a way the optimisations are not: a finished include-or-exclude sweep has visited a superset of the objects it needed to visit, and the two pruning rules above are proved sound, so its verdict depends on no solver and no floating-point arithmetic, only on the checker. Where a value in this thesis is called \emph{proved}, that is usually the reason. Every reported connectivity is exact and integral, a single integer maximum flow, so no extremal value is rounded; real numbers enter only in the fractional optimisation of \Cref{thm:dir-multi-small}, whose reported optimum is a $\{0,1\}$ matrix and is checked exactly. The load-bearing computations are also run twice by implementations that share no code. Agreement is not a proof, but guards against a correct method implemented wrongly. \Cref{app:verification-ladder,app:software-tests} records the evidence.

What this does not amount to is a formally checkable SAT-style certificate, such as a resolution proof a small independent verifier could replay. That standard would have been appropriate for $L_3^{\mathrm{dir}}(7)=24$ had it remained a solver question. It did not: \Cref{app:proofs} proves the statement, and its general case for every $n,m$, by hand, so nothing in the thesis rests on an infeasibility verdict without a proof behind it.

\section{The trichotomy: proved, certified, conjectured}

The program keeps three evidential states separate. Exhaustion can \emph{prove} a finite value; the cut-counting optimisation can \emph{certify} a finite upper bound; and a heuristic search can only \emph{conjecture}, by supplying a feasible witness and hence a lower bound. A value is called settled only when its lower and upper bounds meet.

The paired grids apply the same reading to all twelve variants. Blue curves are theorems, red curves are conjectured formulas, and yellow curves only interpolate known lower bounds; filled squares are proved or certified finite values and open circles are witnesses. The directed vertex panels were recomputed under the vertex test rather than inherited from the arc case, because Whitney's inequality transfers witnesses but not upper bounds. Early points can be the trivial complete object, and later open circles may come from a named construction rather than the heuristic itself. Direction produces the quadratic panels, while raising $m$ from $3$ to $6$ changes their scale without changing which results are proved.

\begin{computernote}[Honesty]
A number found by the search is reported as a lower bound, and is marked as certified or proved only when a separate cut-counting optimisation or a hand proof supplies the matching upper bound. No extremal value is ever asserted on the strength of the search alone. The search figures use fixed random seeds, so every search point is reproducible.
\end{computernote}
\nowreads
\section{Results and evidential status}

The checker \emph{measures} a supplied graph exactly; completed exhaustion \emph{proves} a finite optimum; the historical cut-counting optimisation is an independent finite check unless accompanied by a replayable bound; and heuristic search supplies only a witness and lower bound. A value is settled only when its lower and upper bounds meet. All computational claims rerun from \code{program/erdos915\_unified.py}; critical values are cross-checked by independent implementations, while \Cref{app:verification-ladder,app:software-tests} records the environment, commands, and audit evidence. No general theorem rests on an unauditable solver status.
\end{offcutrewrite}

\begin{offcutrewrite}{G3. Expanded classical machinery}{G3}{chapters/app\_proofs.tex, Sections A.1--A.2}{the opening of Appendix A.1}{The statements, citations, figures, lemmas, and compact Mader proof remain live. These paragraphs re-explained standard flow, uncrossing, and the information already visible in the figures.}
\noindent\emph{Former appendix opening and reading guide.}

This appendix holds the arguments deferred from the body: the ones that are either standard machinery or long case-checking, and whose length, not whose difficulty, kept them out of the narrative. The body carries the ideas. Here they are carried out in full. Because graphs are visual objects, the longer arguments here are accompanied by figures: each is drawn rather than merely described, and the prose points the reader to the picture at the moment it is needed.

\paragraph{How to read this appendix.} The sections divide into standard machinery and the thesis's own proofs, and a reader chasing one thread of the body need not read all of them. The first two sections, on \Cref{thm:menger}, \Cref{thm:gomory-hu}, and Mader's theorem, are classical results included only so the body can cite them in the exact form it uses, and a reader who already trusts Menger, Gomory--Hu, and Mader may skip them. Six sections carry the load-bearing original arguments, and they fall into two groups. Three are about the directed models. The directed arc value at $m = 2$ supplies the finite base cases the body's induction rests on. The structural propositions on the directed frontier prove the unconditional quadratic bound, and then isolate the one counting fact behind it (\Cref{lem:two-step-budget}), which is what the last of the six reuses. The directed multigraph problem for all $n$ and $m$ is the longest argument here and the one behind the main directed contribution. Three more are about the other two models. The hypergraph vertex problem proves the incidence-rank lemma that gives the universal $m=3$ upper bound and, in the stated attainment range, the exact value. The multigraph vertex problem under the other convention withdraws a natural guess and replaces it with a construction and a matching order of growth. The directed hypergraph settles a leading constant, and is the section to read first for what \Cref{lem:two-step-budget} is good for beyond the case it was proved for. The remaining section on the hypergraph edge bounds supports a single claim of the body and can be read on its own when that claim is reached. Finally, the computational transcripts record the outputs on which finite claims rely, while \Cref{app:code} identifies the versioned source, commands, and tests needed to audit or reproduce them.

\noindent\emph{Former opening and Menger application paragraph.}

The two classical theorems below are the foundation of everything the thesis measures, and we state them at the level of generality at which the program actually applies them. A \emph{capacitated graph}, or \emph{weighted graph}, is a graph, directed or not, carrying a non-negative capacity $c(e)$ on each edge or arc. A plain graph is read as the \emph{unit-capacity} case $c\equiv1$, and a multigraph as the integer-capacity case, one unit per parallel copy. A \emph{flow} from $u$ to $v$ sends units along edges without exceeding any capacity, and the capacity of a cut is the total capacity crossing it. At unit and integer capacities maximum flow is the connectivity $\lec$.

Every graph model the thesis uses is a special case of a directed capacitated graph: an undirected graph is the symmetric one, each edge a pair of opposite unit-capacity arcs; a multigraph is the integer-capacity one, with $\mu(u,v)$ units on the pair $uv$; and the hypergraph version is the theorem applied to the helper-point network of \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget}. The internally vertex-disjoint version uses vertex splitting: replace each internal vertex $w$ by two copies joined by a unit-capacity arc $w^-\to w^+$, send entering arcs into $w^-$ and leaving arcs out of $w^+$. A flow can then pass through $w$ at most once, so edge-disjoint paths in the split graph are internally vertex-disjoint paths in the original. This is why one maximum-flow routine measures all four separations.

\noindent\emph{Former Gomory--Hu scope and existence discussion.}

Being a theorem about capacities, Gomory--Hu applies verbatim to a simple graph, a multigraph, and, in the form of \Cref{thm:hyper-gomory-hu}, to hyperedge connectivity. But it needs the cut function to be symmetric, which forces the graph to be undirected: a digraph has $\lec(u,v)\ne\lec(v,u)$ in general and admits no such tree, and vertex connectivity has no corresponding cut tree. Thus the Gomory--Hu tree drives Mader's theorem and its multigraph and hypergraph cousins, and vanishes once arcs turn one-way.

Existence rests on submodularity. Writing $d(S)$ for the capacity of $(S,V\setminus S)$,
\[
d(A)+d(B)\ge d(A\cap B)+d(A\cup B).
\]
This lets a cheapest cut crossing another set be traded for one that does not, without increasing capacity. The tree is built in $n-1$ minimum-cut rounds, and the uncrossing lemma guarantees that later cuts do not spoil earlier ones. The construction and induction are in Gomory and Hu's original 1961 paper.

The most basic constraint on local connectivity is local: a pair cannot have more independent routes than either endpoint has edges. The constructions below use it constantly.

\noindent\emph{Former Mader-proof introduction and figure explanation.}

The upper bound of \Cref{thm:mader} rests on three short lemmas about a Gomory--Hu tree $T$ of $G$. For an edge $e=\{u,v\}$, write $\dist_T(e)$ for the number of tree edges on the unique $u$--$v$ path. Since $T$ is a tree on the same vertex set, $\dist_T(e)\ge1$. The figure marks the edges with tree distance one and those with larger distance. The first lemma is the one place the proof uses that $G$ is simple, and exactly where the simple bound improves on the multigraph bound.

Former caption for \Cref{fig:gomory-hu-dist}: A simple graph $G$ and a Gomory--Hu tree $T$ on the same five vertices. The tree has edges $ea(2)$, $ac(3)$, $cb(3)$, and $cd(2)$, each weight equal to the local edge connectivity of its endpoints. These four are also graph edges and have $\dist_T(e)=1$; a simple graph admits at most $n-1$ such edges. The remaining graph edges join tree-distance-two pairs. Reading $\dist_T(e)$ as the number of tree cuts an edge crosses makes $\sum_e\dist_T(e)=\sum_t c(t)$. Every edge crosses at least one cut, but only $n-1$ can cross exactly one, producing the factor two in Mader's bound.

In words: the total weight of the tree equals the sum, over graph edges, of how many tree edges each one straddles. A graph edge is counted once for every tree cut separating its endpoints, and $\dist_T(e)$ is precisely that count.

Note where the factor two came from: every edge crosses at least one tree cut, but a simple graph can afford at most $n-1$ edges that cross only one, so on average each edge is charged closer to twice. For multigraphs that refinement is unavailable, since parallel edges between tree-adjacent pairs each have $\dist_T=1$, and the same inequalities stop at the multigraph bound $(m-1)(n-1)$.

The lower bound is a pair of constructions, drawn side by side. The first is the shape the bound was guessed from and works when $m-1$ divides $n-1$; the second generalises it to every $n\ge m\ge2$.

Former subcaptions: ``Shared-clique graph: three $K_4$'s glued at one hub $h$ ($m=4$, $k=3$). Each non-hub vertex has degree $m-1=3$.'' And: ``Hub-and-spokes $H_{m,n}$: the hub edges $\{h,v_i\}$ (light) plus a $(m-2)$-regular spoke graph (dark) in two $K_{m-1}=K_3$ cliques.''

Former main caption: The two extremal families. The shared-clique graph works when $(m-1)\mid(n-1)$: every pair contains a non-hub vertex of degree exactly $m-1$, while the edge count reaches $m(n-1)/2$. The hub-and-spokes graph $H_{m,n}$ reaches the same bound for every $n\ge m$: each spoke has total degree $1+(m-2)=m-1$, and choosing the spoke graph as disjoint $K_{m-1}$ cliques recovers the first construction. The hub is orange in both.
\nowreads
The live appendix states the capacitated conventions in two sentences, cites the classical constructions, and retains the theorem statements. Its Mader section keeps the three lemmas and proof intact, scales the Gomory--Hu diagram to $86\%$, and lets shorter captions carry only information not immediate from the drawings.
\end{offcutrewrite}

\begin{offcut}{G4. Long-form captions for retained figures}{G4}{chapters/ch1\_basecases.tex and chapters/ch2\_machine.tex}{the corresponding live figure label}{Only caption prose moved: every figure and label remains live. The MILP captions are in G1 and the classical-proof captions in G3.}
\small
\begin{description}
\item[\code{fig:k5-example}]
A simple graph on five vertices. It is $K_5$, the complete graph in which every one of the $\binom{5}{2}=10$ pairs is joined by an edge (the binomial coefficient counts the ways to choose two vertices), with $\{0,4\}$ and $\{1,3\}$ removed, so $E(G)$ holds eight edges. The dots are $V(G)$ and the lines are the edges. Because at most one line joins a pair and no edge loops to its own endpoint, this is simple. The same graph returns in \Cref{fig:menger}.

\item[\code{fig:menger}]
Menger's theorem on the graph of \Cref{fig:k5-example}. The pair $1,3$ carries no edge of its own, yet three routes join them with no shared edge: $1$--$0$--$3$ (orange), $1$--$2$--$3$ (green), and $1$--$4$--$3$ (purple). The three edges meeting vertex $1$ inside the red ring form a smallest cut. No two edges can cut all three disjoint routes, so both the route packing and cheapest cut have size three.

\item[\code{fig:three-models}]
The first axis of variation, the model, shown for the four object classes. In a simple graph each pair has at most one edge. A multigraph permits parallel edges, so $\mu(u,v)$ counts copies. A hypergraph replaces pairwise edges by sets of $r\ge2$ vertices, drawn like metro lines through their stations; shared stretches are drawn side by side. A multi-hypergraph lets a hyperedge repeat, drawn as parallel lines through the same stations. When every edge has size $r$ the hypergraph is $r$-uniform. The same connectivity question is asked of all four.

\item[\code{fig:three-models-directed}]
The four models of \Cref{fig:three-models} redrawn directed. Every edge becomes an arc and a pair may carry arcs both ways. A simple digraph allows one arc per ordered pair; a directed multigraph adds parallel arcs. A directed hyperedge is a metro-styled star whose tail sends an arc to every head. Taking the middle station as tail keeps the arcs on the same edges as before. Repeating a directed hyperedge repeats each of its arcs. The last panel is the directed multi-hypergraph at the top of the model lattice.

\item[\code{fig:directed-lambda}]
A directed multigraph on the same five vertices as \Cref{fig:k5-example}. The pair $\{0,4\}$ is joined and $\{0,2\}$ carries two parallel arcs, so this is not merely the earlier graph with arrows. Three coloured arc-disjoint routes connect $u$ to $v$. The red marks cut the three arcs leaving $u$, hence $\lambda(u,v)=3$. A directed cut ignores the lone arc entering $u$. The degree bound is $\min(d^+(u),d^-(v))=\min(3,4)=3$.

\item[\code{fig:edge-vs-vertex}]
The third axis, separation. The orange and green $s$--$t$ routes share no edge, so $\lec(s,t)=2$. Both pass through $w$, so they are not internally vertex-disjoint, and every $s$--$t$ route crosses $w$, giving $\kap(s,t)=1$. This makes Whitney's inequality concrete.

\item[\code{fig:bipartite-digraph}]
The one-directional complete bipartite digraph $B_{4,4}$ at $n=8$. Every one of its $|A||B|=\floor{n^2/4}$ arcs runs from $A$ to $B$; none lies inside a part or returns from $B$ to $A$. The only route from $a_i$ to $b_j$ is the direct arc, so every pair has local edge connectivity at most one while the arc count grows quadratically.

\item[\code{fig:bidirected-star}]
The directed hub at $n=6$ for two values of $m$. Left ($m=2$): the bidirected star has $2(n-1)=10$ arcs and all spoke communication passes through $h$, so $\lec^{\max}=1$. Right ($m=3$): adding the orange cycle $0\to1\to2\to3\to4\to0$ makes each spoke satisfy $d^+=d^-=2$. The total is $m(n-1)=15$ and $\lec^{\max}=2$.

\item[\code{fig:augmented-bipartite}]
The thirty-arc counterexample at $m=3$, $n=10$, $|A|=|B|=5$. The $25$ faint arcs form the complete layer $A\to B$ and the five orange arcs form a directed cycle inside $B$. The heavy blue direct arc and green two-step detour give two arc-disjoint routes from $a_1$ to $b_2$. Thus $\lec^{\max}=2$, while $30>\max(3\cdot9,25)=27$.

\item[\code{fig:hyper-star}]
The star hypertree attaining the $m=2$ hypergraph bound, here $r=3$ and $n=9$. The hub lies on every line, and each of the $\floor{(n-1)/(r-1)}=4$ hyperedges hangs a fresh block of two vertices from it. Every two lines meet only at $h$, so no pair has two hyperedge-disjoint routes and Berge connectivity is one.

\item[\code{fig:representation}]
How the program stores a graph. A graph or digraph on $n$ vertices is an $n\times n$ integer matrix $\mu$, with $\mu(u,v)$ the multiplicity of $u\to v$. The diagonal is zero, an undirected graph has $\mu=\mu^\top$, a directed graph need not, and a simple graph caps entries at one. A hypergraph cannot use the matrix and is stored as a list of hyperedges, shown as metro lines.

\item[\code{fig:prune}]
Branch and bound over digraphs. Each node fixes one more cell, so leaves are complete digraphs. A partial graph already above the connectivity ceiling is infeasible together with all completions (red). A branch whose best completion cannot beat the incumbent is dropped on the counting bound (orange). The surviving branches reach the base cases $n\le7$ that blind enumeration cannot.

\item[\code{fig:complexity}]
The number of graphs blind enumeration must visit, on a logarithmic vertical axis, for directed and undirected models. Each panel shows the simple model, two multiplicity caps, and the $3$-uniform hypergraph, with a dashed $10^9$ budget. A simple model offers $2^E$ candidates, a capped multigraph $m^E$, and a $3$-uniform hypergraph $2^{\binom n3}$ undirected or $2^{n\binom{n-1}{2}}$ directed. The count depends on model and direction, not on separation. Every curve crosses the budget at single-digit $n$, with directed objects first.

\item[\code{fig:trace}]
A cooling run on the directed multigraph problem at $n=4$, $m=3$. Left is the geometric temperature schedule; right plots visited graphs by $\lambda^{\max}$ and arc count, coloured by step. While hot the walk enters the infeasible region $\lambda^{\max}>2$. Cooling drives it back to the certified optimum of $12$ arcs at $\lambda^{\max}=2$.

\item[\code{fig:sensitivity}]
A directed multigraph on eight vertices with $\lambda^{\max}=9$, every parallel arc drawn and coloured by sensitivity $\sigma$. Both $s\to a$ and $s\to b$ carry three copies, yet the former is cool ($\sigma=1$) because $a\to t$ throttles the route, while the latter is hot ($\sigma=3$). Arcs $s\to d$ have $\sigma=0$ because $d$ is a dead end. This tells the cooling search which arcs are load-bearing.

\item[\code{tab:sa-vs-tabu}]
Simulated annealing against tabu search at equal six-second budgets, all at $m=3$. The simple undirected and directed multigraph targets are proved; the simple directed target is the best known value past the exhausted range. Tabu reaches all three targets, while SA plateaus below both directed ones. The final columns give reference-machine time to target.

\item[\code{fig:variant-bounds-comparison}]
All twelve variants, with edges against $n$, at $m=3$ and $m=6$. Rows are simple graphs, multigraphs, and $3$-uniform hypergraphs; columns are undirected edge, undirected vertex, directed arc, and directed vertex separation. Blue curves are proved values, red curves conjectured formulas, and yellow curves interpolations through lower bounds. Filled squares are exact finite sizes; open circles are the better of search and a named construction. The pairing shows the scale change without repeating a full-page grid.
\end{description}
\end{offcut}


\clearpage
\section*{Group H: single-case exhibits and redundant reference material}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Group H: single-case exhibits and redundant reference material}
\markboth{Group H: single-case exhibits}{Group H: single-case exhibits}

\begin{offcut}{H1. The introductory twelve-variant tree}{H1}{chapters/ch1\_basecases.tex, former Figure 1.7 and its lead-in}{the paragraph defining the three axes}{The status tree in Chapter 3 redraws the same branches and adds information.}
Three models, two directions, two separations: twelve versions of one question. The thesis is the attempt to answer as many of them as honest mathematics allows, and to build a machine for the rest. Some answers are short and we prove them here. Some answers are long, and their length is the reason \Cref{ch:certify} exists. The rest of this chapter collects the variants whose proofs are short enough to belong in the body, so that the reader meets the easy slope before the climb. \Cref{fig:variant-tree} draws the whole space as one tree, so that the reader has a single map to return to as the chapters visit its branches one at a time.

\begin{offcut}{H2. The worked 2-tree growth picture}{H2}{chapters/ch1\_basecases.tex, former Figure 1.8 and expanded lead-in}{the paragraph defining a \(k\)-tree}{The recursive definition is enough; the three-step picture handled only one example.}
At $m = 5$ the vertex problem genuinely diverges from the edge problem, visible in \Cref{fig:divergence} as the shaded gap that opens between the dashed blue and solid orange curves. Leonard had already found the first crack, an explicit $57$-vertex, $141$-edge counterexample to the general conjecture at $m = 5$ [Leonard 1973], before S{\o}rensen and Thomassen pinned the exact value down. The reason the crack opens at all is structural. Leonard's earlier method, the one that closes $m \le 4$, leans on the extremal graphs being assembled from cliques, vertex sets joined in all pairs, glued along shared cliques. A \emph{$k$-tree} is built up one vertex at a time: start from a complete graph on $k+1$ vertices, then again and again add a single new vertex and join it to all $k$ vertices of some clique of size $k$ that is already there. \Cref{fig:k-tree} grows a $2$-tree, hanging each new vertex on an edge already present. These clique scaffolds are exactly the building blocks the method uses. Through $m = 4$ those scaffolds are forced, and counting their edges gives the bound.

\centering

Vertex $4$ is then joined to the two ends of the edge $\{2,3\}$, and vertex $5$ to the two ends of the edge $\{3,4\}$ (the new vertex and its two orange edges at each step), each new vertex hung on an edge already present.
