\begin{offcut}{H3. One cooling trace}{H3}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former temperature-trace figure and claim}{the sensitivity-removal paragraph}{A single run is not representative evidence, and the algorithm already states the behaviour.}
When the search proposes a removal it draws the edge with probability proportional to $e^{-\sigma(e) / T}$. The dial is now explicit. At high temperature these weights flatten and the walk toggles edges regardless of how load-bearing they are, exploring widely and willing to dismantle essential structure. As the temperature falls the weights concentrate on $\sigma = 0$, so the walk almost never disturbs a load-bearing edge and instead rearranges only the free ones, until the graph crystallises onto an extremal shape. This is exactly the behaviour one wants, and it is the behaviour the formulation asks for: the temperature tracks how strongly removing an edge changes the bound. \Cref{fig:trace} shows one such run settling onto a certified optimum, and runs across the other matrix models, both directions and the vertex separation behave the same way.

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{figures/temperature_trace.png}
    \caption{A single cooling run on the directed multigraph problem at $n = 4$, $m = 3$. \emph{Left:} the temperature schedule, cooling geometrically from its starting value. \emph{Right:} every visited graph plotted by its binding connectivity $\lambda^{\max}$ (horizontal) against its arc count (vertical), coloured by the step at which it was visited. Annealing uses a soft penalty rather than a hard wall, so while hot (dark points) the walk strays into the infeasible region $\lambda^{\max} > m - 1 = 2$, picking up extra arcs it is not allowed to keep. As the temperature falls (bright points) the penalty drives the walk back across the feasibility boundary and onto the densest graph that stays feasible, the certified optimum of $12$ arcs at $\lambda^{\max} = 2$ (star).}
    \label{fig:trace}
\end{figure}
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{H4. The complete small-\(n\) landscape}{H4}{chapters/ch4\_synthesis.tex, former labelled-graph scatter figure and interpretation}{the end of Two principal phenomena}{The full population plot is a one-case enumeration exhibit rather than part of the synthesis argument.}
That same scarcity of extremal graphs is the thread through the remaining figures. \Cref{fig:scatter-landscape} shows where the frontier runs: it plots every labelled graph at small $n$ as a single point, its binding connectivity $\lambda^{\max}$ on the horizontal axis and its edge count on the vertical, so the whole population of graphs is visible at once. The points crowd into the low-connectivity, low-edge corner and thin out upward, where the blue extremal envelope, an integer staircase carrying one dot per connectivity level, traces the densest graph available at each level. The empty region above that staircase is precisely the part the main theorems forbid a graph to occupy. The extremal problem is the question of where that staircase runs, and the figure shows it is a thin frontier with almost nothing pressed against it from below.

Pooling the same enumeration by individual vertex pair and by sheer edge count tells the same story from two more angles: high-connectivity pairs are rare even inside the graphs that have one, and the densest low-connectivity graphs, the ones the extremal problem is about, sit far out in the sparse tail in every variant.

Finally, \Cref{fig:surface-3d} lifts the whole study onto the $(n, m)$ grid at once, drawing the best feasible edge count as a bar surface across every variant. The four proved variants rise as smooth linear or quadratic sheets that the formulas describe exactly, while the open variants show a visible step between the machine-proved blue bars and the search's purple lower bounds, so the surface makes the proved-versus-open divide of the trichotomy a single readable shape. Reading along the $m$ axis recovers the linear scaling in the threshold, and reading along $n$ recovers each variant's growth rate, which is the whole of \Cref{tab:summary} compressed into one object.

\begin{figure}[p]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/scatter_lambda_edges.png}
    \caption{The complete landscape of all labelled graphs at small $n$ (see panel annotations for each variant's $n$): every point is one graph, plotted at its binding connectivity $\lambda^{\max}$ horizontally and its edge count vertically. The blue staircase marking the densest graph at each connectivity level is the extremal envelope, the frontier the extremal problem asks about, with one dot per level so its exact value is legible. Graphs crowd the low-connectivity, low-edge region. The extremal graphs live at the top of the staircase. The emptiness above the envelope is exactly the region the main theorems forbid. The horizontal reach differs by panel, because the connectivity that fits in a variant's enumeration $n$ differs. Arc-disjoint multigraph routes grow with edge multiplicity, so the multigraph directed arc panel reaches $\lambda^{\max} = 10$ at $n = 3$. Vertex-disjoint routes are capped instead by the available internal vertices, so the multigraph directed \emph{vertex} panel reaches only $\kappa^{\max} = 2$ at that same $n = 3$: with a single internal vertex, a pair has at most the direct route plus one detour. This is a property of the separation, not of the search.}
    \label{fig:scatter-landscape}
\end{figure}
\end{offcut}

        vertex & hyper  & $\floorfrac{n-1}{r-1}$ ($m=2$), $\le\floorfrac{2(n-1)}{r-1}$ ($m=3$) & exact at $m=2$. At $m=3$, universal upper bound, with equality for $r\ge3$, $2\le\binom{n-2}{r-2}$ (\Cref{thm:hyper-vertex-m2,thm:hyper-vertex-m3}), open otherwise \\
        \midrule
        \multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{Directed}} \\
        arc    & simple & $\max(2(n-1),\floor{n^2/4})$ ($m=2$), $n^2/4 + \Theta_m(n)$ ($m\ge3$) & proved (\Cref{thm:dir-arc-m2-exact,thm:dir-arc-linear-error}), exact formula conjectured for $m\ge3$ \\
        arc    & multi  & $(m-1)\max(2(n-1),\floor{n^2/4})$ & proved, all $n$ and $m$ \\
        arc    & hyper  & $(1+o(1))(m-1)n^2/(4(r-1))$, exact value open & leading constant proved (\Cref{thm:dir-hyper-constant}) \\
        vertex & simple & $\max(2(n-1),\floor{n^2/4})$ ($m=2$), $n^2/4 + \Theta_m(n)$ ($m\ge3$) & proved separately (\Cref{thm:dir-vertex-m2-exact,thm:dir-vertex-linear-error}), exact formula conjectured for $m\ge3$ \\
        vertex & multi  & adjacency count, so the simple digraph value & convention (\Cref{sec:parallel-convention}) \\
        vertex & hyper  & $(1+o(1))(m-1)n^2/(4(r-1))$, exact value open & leading constant proved (\Cref{thm:dir-hyper-constant}) \\
        \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}%
    }
    \caption{The twelve structural variants of Problem 915 and their status. Every simple-graph closed form here is stated for $n \ge m$, the hypothesis \Cref{thm:mader}, \Cref{thm:leonard} and \Cref{conj:dir-arc} carry: below it the complete graph or digraph is itself feasible and the answer is the trivial maximum, which the formulas exceed. ``conj.''\ means a conjecture with a proved matching lower bound. ``Leading constant proved'' means the $n^2$ term of the value is a theorem while the exact value is open, which is the strongest thing known about the two directed hypergraph rows. ``Convention'' marks the two rows whose value is fixed by the counting convention of \Cref{sec:parallel-convention} rather than by a theorem. In the undirected hypergraph rows, a displayed inequality is a universal upper bound and the Status column states the ranges in which a construction proves equality. The twelve-panel program's hyper enumeration counts only simple hypergraphs, so a cell outside those ranges remains open even when the same bound is attained by a multihypergraph in a divisibility case. The appearance threshold $p^* = m/n$ of \Cref{thm:gnp-threshold} is deliberately not a column here: it is proved for the edge separation in $G(n,p)$ and extended to its undirected vertex separation in \Cref{rem:threshold-analogues}. No directed threshold is claimed, and the hypergraph models have a different natural density scale (\Cref{rem:threshold-scope}).}

\begin{offcutrewrite}{H8. Mader lower-bound constructions and picture}{H8}{chapters/app\_proofs.tex, former Figure A.2 and construction discussion}{the Mader upper-bound proof}{The hub-and-spokes construction is general; the clique graph is simply its divisible special case, so the two-panel picture and duplicate setup were unnecessary.}
The lower bound is a pair of constructions, drawn side by side in \Cref{fig:mader-lower}. The first is the shape the bound was guessed from, and works when $m - 1$ divides $n - 1$. The second generalises it to every $n \ge m \ge 2$.

\begin{construction}[Shared-clique graph]\offcutlabel{const:shared-clique}
For $m \ge 3$ with $(m-1) \mid (n-1)$, take $k = (n-1)/(m-1)$ copies of the complete graph $K_m$ and identify one vertex of each copy into a single shared hub vertex $h$. The result has $k(m-1) + 1 = n$ vertices and $k \binom{m}{2} = \frac{m(n-1)}{2}$ edges. Every non-hub vertex has degree $m - 1$, and any pair of vertices contains a non-hub vertex, so $\lec^{\max} \le m-1$ by \Cref{lem:degree-bound}.
\end{construction}

\begin{construction}[Hub-and-spokes graph]\offcutlabel{const:hub-spokes}
For $n \ge m \ge 2$, let $H_{m,n}$ have vertex set $\{h, v_1, \ldots, v_{n-1}\}$ with
(i) all $n-1$ \emph{hub edges} $\{h, v_i\}$, and
(ii) a \emph{spoke graph} on $\{v_1, \ldots, v_{n-1}\}$ with $\floorfrac{(m-2)(n-1)}{2}$ edges in which every $v_i$ has spoke degree at most $m-2$. Such a spoke graph exists by \Cref{lem:near-regular}.
\end{construction}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.46\textwidth}
\centering

% hub at centre, three K_4 each sharing the hub.  m=4 => K_4.
\node[gvhub, minimum size=5mm] (h) at (0,0) {};
\node[apname] at (0.86,0.52) {$h$};
% three cliques arranged around hub at 90, 210, 330 degrees
\foreach \ang/\name in {90/A, 210/B, 330/C}{
  \begin{scope}[rotate=\ang]
    \coordinate (p1) at (1.5,0.0);
    \coordinate (p2) at (2.6,0.55);
    \coordinate (p3) at (2.6,-0.55);

    \node[apx] (\name2) at (p2) {};
    \node[apx] (\name3) at (p3) {};
    % K_4 edges among hub + 3 outer
    \draw[apgedge] (h)--(\name1) (h)--(\name2) (h)--(\name3);
    \draw[apgedge] (\name1)--(\name2) (\name1)--(\name3) (\name2)--(\name3);
  \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Shared-clique graph: three $K_4$'s glued at one hub $h$
($m=4$, $k=3$). Each non-hub vertex has degree $m-1=3$.}
\label{fig:shared-clique}
\end{subfigure}\hfill
\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.50\textwidth}
\centering

\foreach \i/\ang in {1/90,2/150,3/210,4/270,5/330,6/30}{
  \node[apx] (v\i) at (\ang:2.3) {};
}

\draw[apgedge,KULblauw3b] (v4)--(v5) (v5)--(v6) (v4)--(v6);
\end{tikzpicture}

\label{fig:hub-spokes}
\end{subfigure}
\caption[Lower-bound constructions for Mader's theorem]{The two extremal
families. \textbf{(a)} The shared-clique graph of
\Cref{const:shared-clique} works when $(m-1)\mid(n-1)$: every pair contains a
non-hub vertex of degree exactly $m-1$, so $\lec^{\max}\le m-1$ by the degree
bound, while the edge count hits $\tfrac{m(n-1)}{2}$. \textbf{(b)} The
hub-and-spokes graph $H_{m,n}$ of \Cref{const:hub-spokes} reaches the same
bound for \emph{every} $n\ge m$: each spoke vertex $v_i$ has total degree

\begin{offcutrewrite}{H9. SPQR primer and leaf schematic}{H9}{chapters/app\_proofs.tex, former Figure A.6 and primer}{Step 4 of Lemma A.55}{The decomposition is load-bearing, but the tutorial and schematic were not. The proof now cites Tutte and states only the two leaf properties it uses.}
Otherwise $G$ is $2$-connected but not $3$-connected, so it can be taken
apart at its $2$-vertex separators.

\begin{primer}[Primer: the triconnected (SPQR) decomposition]
A $2$-connected graph that is not $3$-connected must have a separating pair, and
cutting along every such pair sorts any $2$-connected graph into a few standard
shapes. This is a classical theorem of Tutte [1966], made into a
linear-time algorithm by Hopcroft and Tarjan [1973].

\emph{Theorem (triconnected decomposition).} Every $2$-connected graph $G$ on at
least three vertices decomposes into a \emph{tree of pieces}, unique up to
relabelling, in which each piece is one of three kinds: a \emph{cycle} of length
at least three (an $S$-piece, for \emph{series}), a \emph{bond}, meaning two
vertices joined by three or more parallel edges (a $P$-piece, for \emph{parallel}),
or a $3$-connected simple graph on at least four vertices (an $R$-piece, for
\emph{rigid}). Two adjacent pieces share exactly one \emph{virtual edge}
$\{a, b\}$, a placeholder that records a separating pair of $G$ at which the two
pieces were split apart. The three initials $S$, $P$, $R$, together with $Q$ for a
trivial single-edge piece, give the structure its usual name, the \emph{SPQR
tree}.

The tree is built by the recursion that also defines it. If $G$ is already a
cycle, a bond, or $3$-connected, it is a single piece and the tree is one node.
Otherwise choose a separating pair $\{a, b\}$, split $G$ into the parts that this
pair separates, add the virtual edge $\{a, b\}$ to each part so the split is
remembered, and recurse on the parts. Every split makes the parts strictly
smaller, so the recursion halts, and Tutte's theorem is that the resulting
collection of pieces does not depend on the order in which the separating pairs
are taken. The one fact the proof draws from all this is that a virtual edge
$\{a, b\}$ always stands for a genuine $a$--$b$ path through the rest of $G$, so an
argument that must cross from $a$ to $b$ outside a given piece can always do so on
the far side of that piece's virtual edge.
\end{primer}

The decomposition just stated is sketched in \Cref{fig:tutte-leaf}, which also
marks the leaf at which the argument bites. Two properties are all the proof needs
from it, and both are visible in the figure. First, a vertex of a piece
that touches none of that piece's virtual edges belongs to that piece alone,
so it keeps in $G$ exactly the degree it has in the piece. Second, for any
virtual edge $\{a, b\}$ the part of $G$ on the far side of the cut contains a
genuine $a$--$b$ path whose interior avoids the piece, so the virtual edge
can always be replaced by a real route.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
% Two blobs sharing the cut pair {a,b}: the far side ("rest of G", left) and
% the 3-connected leaf L (right), joined only by the virtual edge {a,b}.
\node[draw=KULblauw3b, fill=KULblauw1!8, line width=1pt,
      ellipse, minimum width=3.3cm, minimum height=3.5cm] (R) at (0,0) {};
\node[font=\small, KULblauw3b] at (0,-2.1) {rest of $G$};
\node[draw=vertexpurple, fill=vertexpurple!9, line width=1.2pt,
      ellipse, minimum width=3.3cm, minimum height=3.5cm] (L) at (5.4,0) {};
\node[font=\small, vertexpurple] at (5.4,2.15) {leaf $L$ (3-connected)};
% the shared cut pair a,b on the seam between the two blobs
\node[apx] (a) at (2.7, 1.3) {};  \node[apname, above=4pt] at (a) {$a$};
\node[apx] (b) at (2.7,-1.3) {};  \node[apname, below=4pt] at (b) {$b$};
% virtual edge {a,b}: the leaf's single connection to the rest of G
\draw[apdashed] (a)--(b);
\node[apname, text=vertexred, fill=white, inner sep=1.5pt] at (2.7,0) {virtual $\{a,b\}$};
% inside L: vertices and edges suggesting 3-connectivity, with a far node z*
\node[apx] (z1) at (5.0, 1.0) {};
\node[apx] (z2) at (6.15, 0.0) {};  \node[apname, right=3pt] at (z2) {$z^{*}$};
\node[apx] (z3) at (5.0,-1.0) {};
\draw[apgedge] (a)--(z1) (a)--(z2) (a)--(z3) (b)--(z1) (b)--(z2) (b)--(z3)
               (z1)--(z2) (z2)--(z3) (z1)--(z3);
% far-side real a--b path through the rest of G, avoiding L (green)
\draw[apgedge, vertexgreen!72!black, line width=1.7pt]
   (a) to[out=185,in=72] (-1.2,0.1) to[out=-72,in=175] (b);
\node[apname, text=vertexgreen!56!black, align=center] at (0.55,-0.55)
   {far-side real\\$a$--$b$ path};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[Tutte leaf analysis]{The triconnected (Tutte/SPQR) decomposition in
Step 4 of \Cref{lem:incidence-rank}, schematic: $G$ splits into a tree of
pieces (blobs) glued along virtual edges, and the analysis works at a
\emph{leaf} $L$ with its single virtual edge $\{a,b\}$ (dashed red). The far
side of the tree supplies a \emph{real} $a$--$b$ path (green) that internally
avoids $L$. If $L$ is $3$-connected, any two of its vertices are joined by
three internally disjoint paths inside $L$, at most one using the virtual
edge, and the far-side path replaces it, so $\kap_G\ge3$ for every pair in
$L$. With $|V(L)|\ge4$ that forces a hyperedge-node $z^*\notin\{a,b\}$ of
degree at most one, which is the contradiction that closes the lemma.}
\label{fig:tutte-leaf}
\end{figure}
\nowreads
The live proof retains one compact paragraph invoking Tutte's triconnected decomposition and then carries out the leaf contradiction.
\end{offcutrewrite}

\caption[Drawing a hyperedge]{How the thesis draws a single hyperedge on its $r$
vertices, in the metro colours of \Cref{fig:three-models}. \emph{Left:} an
undirected hyperedge is one metro line threading all its members, with no arrows.
\emph{Right:} a directed hyperedge, a tail with $r-1$ heads, is drawn as a star,
the tail sending one thick arc to each head, so a Berge route enters at the tail
and leaves at any head. The hypergraph panels of \Cref{fig:three-models-directed}
use this same star. Where a directed hyperedge instead has to sit on a metro line
it already occupies undirected, as in the directed whole-hypergraph example, the
line is oriented along its own length away from the tail rather than fanned out,
which draws the same object with fewer crossings. Either way one rule recovers the
tail from the picture: it is the single station of that line into which none of its
own arrows points. The shape is a drawing convention, not a path. Each coloured line
or star is a \emph{single} edge on several vertices, not a sequence of ordinary
edges, so the line on the left is one hyperedge and not three. One colour means
one hyperedge, and where two of them meet they cross at a shared station rather
than joining into a longer line. What might be mistaken for a hyperedge is a
\emph{route}, which does run edge by edge from one vertex to another, but a route
is drawn as a thin thread laid over the lines, so the thick coloured hyperedge
and the thin route never look alike.}
\label{fig:dir-hyper-convention}
\end{figure}
\end{offcut}

\medskip
The reduction changes what has to be searched. Instead of all feasible multigraphs on $n$ vertices, one enumerates $2$-connected simple graphs on $b \le n$ vertices, which \code{geng} does directly, evaluates $W_m$ on each, and solves a small knapsack. Every value in \Cref{tab:multi-vertex-exact} was produced that way, over all $2$-connected graphs on at most eight vertices, and every cell the exhaustive routine had previously proved agrees with it.

    $m=6$ & $5$ & $12$ & $19$ & $26$ & $33$ & $40$ & $47$ \\
    $m=7$ & $6$ & $15$ & $24$ & $33$ & $42$ & $52$ & $62$ \\
    $m=8$ & $7$ & $18$ & $30$ & $42$ & $54$ & $66$ & $79$ \\
\end{tabular}
\caption{$K_m^{\mathrm{multi}}(n)$, exact, from \Cref{thm:multi-vertex-blocks} together with an exhaustive sweep of the $2$-connected graphs on at most eight vertices ($1$, $1$, $3$, $10$, $56$, $468$ and $7123$ of them). The knapsack shows a clean split. For $m \le 3$ the optimum is a thickened tree, every block a single edge. At $m = 4$ the two agree, every split of the vertices scoring the same. In the displayed range, from $m = 5$ on and for $n\ge3$, a single $2$-connected block strictly beats every split, so the extremiser is $2$-connected and the bouquet is a construction rather than the answer. The bold cell is the one \Cref{thm:clique-chain-vertex} reaches only $27$ on and an unfinished search had found $28$ on. As a check on the reduction itself, the exhaustive routine behind \Cref{tab:multi-vertex}, which shares no reasoning with the block argument, was run against these values: it proves $23$ of them within its time limit and agrees on every one, and the cells where it runs out of time return lower bounds that are consistent, the bold one among them.}
\label{tab:multi-vertex-exact}
\end{table}
\end{offcut}

\section*{Group I: implementation listings and the directed duplicate}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Group I: implementation listings and directed duplicate}
\markboth{Group I: implementation listings}{Group I: implementation listings}

\begin{offcut}{I1. Matrix-assignment helper}{I1}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Listing 2.1}{after the graph-operations table}{The prose states the symmetry invariant directly; the implementation remains in the software archive.}
The two booleans are all the mirroring above needs, and the whole trick fits in one short routine. Every write to $\mu$ passes through it, so this is the single place the undirected symmetry is kept, not an invariant a caller has to remember to maintain.

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={How every write keeps an undirected graph symmetric. A directed graph simply skips the mirrored write.}, label={offcut:lst:assign}]
def _assign(self, u, v, value):
    self.mu[u, v] = value
    if not self.variant.directed:
        self.mu[v, u] = value   # keep undirected graphs symmetric
\end{lstlisting}
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{I2. Hyperedge-gate loop}{I2}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Listing 2.2}{the directed-hypergraph checker}{The surrounding text and diagrams already specify the reduction.}
The gate is built once per hyperedge, directed or not, by the same loop:

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The gate loop behind \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget} and \Cref{fig:dir-hyper-gadget}: one capacity-one gate per hyperedge, wired one-way for a directed hyperedge and both ways for an undirected one.}, label={offcut:i:lst:gate}]
gate_in, gate_out = base + 2 * index, base + 2 * index + 1
cap[gate_in, gate_out] = 1           # one route through this hyperedge
if hypergraph.directed:
    tails, heads = _dir_tails_heads(edge)
    for tail in tails:
        cap[leave(tail), gate_in] = _UNBOUNDED
    for head in heads:
        cap[gate_out, enter(head)] = _UNBOUNDED
\end{lstlisting}
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{I3. Capped-energy branch}{I3}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Listing 2.3}{after Proposition 2.13}{The proposition and surrounding prose already establish the three cases.}
The three cases of \Cref{prop:monotone} read directly off the code:

\begin{lstlisting}[caption={The three cases behind \Cref{prop:monotone}, in the order the proof gives them: removal from a feasible graph (part 1), addition below the bound (part 2), and only otherwise a single capped flow.}, label={offcut:lst:capped-energy}]
if (is_add and lam_ub <= m - 2) or (not is_add and feasible_current):
    excess = 0                # part (2) or part (1): provably feasible
elif not exceeds_bound(proposal, m - 1, separation=separation):
    excess = 0                # at the boundary, but the flow clears it
else:
    excess = measure_full(proposal) - (m - 1)   # infeasible: exact penalty
\end{lstlisting}
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{I4. Directed whole-hypergraph duplicate}{I4}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Figure 2.5}{after the three checker gadgets}{The undirected whole-hypergraph example already shows the gate-disjoint routes; the small directed gate diagram supplies the only additional rule.}
Former live lead-in: the directed whole-hypergraph example orients the same hypergraph. Only two of the four undirected routes survive, because $v$ is the tail of only two hyperedges. Adding ordinary vertex splitting then measures the directed vertex variant as well, so the one gate construction covers all four hypergraph cases.

Former live caption: The same hypergraph oriented, with the two surviving gate-disjoint routes from $v$ to $u$ drawn in black. The checker reads $\lec(v,u)=2$, half the undirected value of \Cref{fig:hyper-gadget-example}.

The complete TikZ source and expanded caption are preserved earlier in E3 under the local label \code{fig:dir-hyper-gadget-example}; that copy is the same construction at full width and is the restore source for this figure.
\end{offcut}

Run on the small cases this confirms the proved values directly, with no induction and no cleverness. \Cref{tab:bruteforce} collects a spread of variants where the enumeration finishes: in every row the machine returns exactly the value \Cref{ch:basecases} proved by hand. This is the first payoff of the uniform representation. One driver, \code{solve}, pointed at six different problems, settles all six.

Variant & $m$ & $n$ & \code{solve} & Theory \\
\midrule
simple undirected, edge   & 2 & 5 & 4  & $\floor{m(n-1)/2}=4$ \\

\begin{offcut}{I6. Simulated-annealing algorithm}{I6}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Algorithm 1}{the temperature-search section}{This is the standard Metropolis cooling loop; the live prose retains the energy, acceptance rule, cooling, sensitivity bias, and evidential status.}
\begin{algorithm}[t]
\caption{Temperature search for a dense feasible graph}\label{alg:anneal}
\KwIn{vertices $n$, value $m$, start $T_0$, cooling $\alpha$, penalty $\Lambda$, steps $S$}
\KwOut{the densest feasible graph encountered}
$G \gets$ empty graph on $n$ vertices\;
$T \gets T_0$\;
\For{$S$ steps}{
    propose $G'$ by toggling one adjacency, biasing removals toward low-sensitivity edges\;
    $\Delta \gets E(G') - E(G)$\;
    \If{$\Delta \le 0$ \textbf{or} $\mathrm{rand}() < e^{-\Delta / T}$}{
        $G \gets G'$\;
    }
    \If{$G$ is feasible \textbf{and} denser than the best so far}{
        record $G$ as the best\;
    }
    $T \gets \alpha\, T$\;
}
\Return the best recorded graph\;
\end{algorithm}
\end{offcut}

\section{Computational transcripts}\offcutlabel{sec:transcripts}

The directed $m=2$ induction needs the cases $2\le n\le7$. Separate pruned
exhaustive searches under the arc and vertex tests both complete and return
$M(n)=\max(2(n-1),\floor{n^2/4})$ throughout. The second run is necessary
because Whitney's inequality does not transfer an arc upper bound to the
vertex problem (\Cref{rem:whitney-direction}). Full logs are retained as
\path{figures/basecase_search_log.txt} and
\path{figures/basecase_search_vertex_log.txt}.

The cut-counting optimisation of \Cref{ch:certify} is a separate and weaker kind of record. It closes the directed multigraph problem only up to $n = 6$, taking about twenty minutes there, and stalls at $n = 7$ because the solver handles the yes-or-no cut labels by case-splitting and the number of cases grows too fast. Its archived runs reported $M^{*}(n) = 2(n-1)$ with status \texttt{OPTIMAL} for $3 \le n \le 6$, but recorded no independently checkable best bound or gap, so no claim here rests on them: \Cref{thm:dir-multi-full} proves the value for every $n$ and every $m$ by hand. The raw log is kept with the other two in the repository (\Cref{app:software-reproduction}).

The complete commented source is maintained with the document as \code{erdos915\_unified.py} in the \code{program/} directory and should be included in the archived submission described in \Cref{app:code}.
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{J3. Software and reproducibility appendix}{J3}{chapters/app\_code.tex and its former chapter declaration in main.tex}{before the bibliography}{The repository README is the native, maintained audit record; the live thesis retains a concise repository and verification note.}

\section{Recreating the environment}\label{app:software-environment}

The core requires Python 3, NumPy, and SciPy; PuLP, Matplotlib, NetworkX, and \textsc{nauty}/\code{geng} support the optional certifier, figures, Gomory--Hu comparison, and generation pipeline. From the repository root:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pip install -r program/requirements.txt
\end{verbatim}
Record \code{python --version}, \code{python -m pip freeze}, the platform, and every solver's name, version, status, bound, objective, and gap. The optional C accelerator is built with \code{bash program/build\_fast.sh}; it changes performance, not the expected answer.

\section{Running the checks}\offcutlabel{app:software-tests}

From \code{program/}, run
\begin{verbatim}
python erdos915_unified.py
python -m unittest discover -s tests -v
\end{verbatim}
and preserve the complete output, including skips. The suite cross-checks known graph and hypergraph values, Whitney's inequality, independent vertex splitting, the $r=2$ reduction, fast predicates, relabelling invariance, named constructions, and exhaustive base cases. Passing tests detect consistency; they do not replace a proof of a general theorem.

\section{Reproducing figures and finite outputs}\offcutlabel{app:software-reproduction}

Run \code{python make\_figures.py} from \code{program/}. Numerical data and labels, rather than image bytes, are the portable comparison across plotting-library versions. The base-case and certificate transcripts live in \code{figures/}; a valid replication must finish the relevant run, report a zero solver gap where applicable, extract a witness, and recheck its edge count and all local connectivities independently. A checkpoint records progress, not completion.

\chapter{Software and Reproducibility}\label{app:code}
\input{chapters/app_code}

The other convention is perfectly available and asks something genuinely different. A single edge is a path with no interior, so $q$ parallel copies between the same pair are $q$ internally vertex-disjoint paths, and feasibility caps every multiplicity at $m-1$ by itself. Nothing runs away, and the multigraph vertex problem separates from the simple one immediately: at $n = 2$ and $m = 3$ two parallel edges are feasible where the simple graph allows one. That is a new extremal problem rather than a variant of a solved one, and \Cref{ch:synthesis} lists it among the open problems.

Multigraph vertex, parallel copies distinct & Exact block-knapsack formulation and all $n,m\le8$; order $\Theta(m^2n)$ as $m\to\infty$ with $n/m\to\infty$ (\Cref{thm:multi-vertex-blocks,prop:multi-vertex-upper}) & Determine the asymptotic constant and the optimal $2$-connected blocks. \\
\end{lstlisting}

The longer former $r=2$ boundary remark and the introductory block-problem cross-references are preserved in J1 itself, where their target lemmas and theorems remain local and restorable.
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{J5. Program representation and operations displays}{J5}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Section 2.2}{after the solver overview}{Chapter 1 already defines the model axes; the live machine chapter now states only the common data representation needed by later methods.}
\section{A single representation for all variants}
Two of the three axes from \Cref{ch:basecases}, the model and the direction, are properties of the object, and a single data structure holds almost all of them. Of the three structural models, simple graphs and multigraphs fit the matrix directly, while the hypergraph keeps the separate storage promised in \Cref{ch:basecases} and rejoins at the end of this chapter. The hypergraph could in principle be pressed into the same shape as a higher-order tensor, an $r$-dimensional array marking which $r$-sets are present, but that array is almost entirely zero and costs $n^r$ entries, so the program keeps the compact list of hyperedges instead and pays nothing for the empty cells. A graph or digraph on $n$ vertices is stored as an integer matrix $\mu$, where $\mu(u, v)$ is the multiplicity of the edge or arc from $u$ to $v$. A simple graph is the case $\mu \in \{0, 1\}$, an undirected graph satisfies $\mu = \mu^{\top}$, and a multigraph allows larger entries. The third axis, edge against vertex separation, is a property of the question rather than the object, so it lives in how we measure $\mu$, not in $\mu$ itself. \Cref{fig:matrix-rep} illustrates the encoding.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth]
% ---- (a) graph -> multiplicity matrix, diagonal highlighted ----
\node[vertex] (A) at (0,   0)   {$1$};
\node[vertex] (B) at (2.0, 0)   {$2$};
\node[vertex] (C) at (1.0, 1.7) {$3$};
\draw[edge] (A) to[bend left=15]  (B);
\draw[edge] (A) to[bend right=15] (B);
\draw[edge] (B) -- (C);
\draw[edge] (A) -- (C);
\node[font=\small] at (1.0, -0.8) {multigraph $G$};
\node at (3.3, 0.55) {$\xrightarrow{\;\mu\;}$};
\node at (5.4, 0.55) {$\begin{pmatrix} {\color{vertexred}\mathbf 0} & 2 & 1 \\ 2 & {\color{vertexred}\mathbf 0} & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & {\color{vertexred}\mathbf 0} \end{pmatrix}$};
\node[font=\small] at (5.4, -0.8) {multiplicity matrix $\mu$};
\node[font=\scriptsize, vertexred] at (5.4, -1.3) {diagonal always $0$: no loops};
% ---- (b) hypergraph -> list of hyperedges ----
\begin{scope}[yshift=-3.5cm]
  \coordinate (p1) at (0.0, 0.6);
  \coordinate (p2) at (0.0,-0.6);
  \coordinate (p3) at (1.1, 0.0);
  \coordinate (p4) at (2.2, 0.6);
  \coordinate (p5) at (2.2,-0.6);
  \begin{scope}[on background layer]
    \draw[metro, metroA] (p1)--(p3)--(p2);
    \draw[metro, metroB] (p4)--(p3)--(p5);
  \end{scope}
  \foreach \p/\lbl in {p1/1,p2/2,p3/3,p4/4,p5/5} {\node[smallvertex] at (\p) {$\lbl$};}
  \node[font=\small] at (1.1,-1.25) {hypergraph $H$};
  \node at (3.3, 0.0) {$\longrightarrow$};
  \node[font=\small, align=left] at (5.7, 0.0)
    {list of hyperedges:\\[3pt] $e_1=\{1,2,3\}$\\[1pt] $e_2=\{3,4,5\}$};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Program representations. Graphs use the multiplicity matrix $\mu$ (zero diagonal, symmetric when undirected, binary when simple); hypergraphs use a list of vertex sets.}
\label{fig:matrix-rep}
\end{figure}

\codecard{Graph(n, Variant(directed, simple))}{MODEL}
{the vertex count $n$ and a \code{Variant}, the small record holding the two booleans}
{a graph whose whole state is the matrix $\mu$, stored as the \code{numpy} array \code{self.mu}}
{one $n \times n$ integer \code{numpy} array is the object, an undirected graph keeps $\mu=\mu^{\top}$, and a simple graph caps every entry at one.}

Keeping a single representation is what lets one measurement routine, one certifier, and one search serve every case. Nothing downstream asks which of the twelve problems it is solving. It asks only the two booleans (directed or undirected, simple or multi) and the matrix. Everything that builds or edits a graph is a thin wrapper around $\mu$ that respects those booleans, and these operations are so routine that they need no individual exposition, and \Cref{tab:graph-ops} lists them together.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
\toprule
Operation & Meaning \\
\midrule
\code{addEdge(G, u, v, k)}        & add $k$ parallel edges or arcs $u \to v$ (a simple graph saturates at one) \\
\code{removeEdge(G, u, v, k)}     & remove $k$ copies, never below zero \\
\code{setMultiplicity(G, u, v, c)}& set $\mu(u,v) = c$ directly \\
\code{multiplicity(G, u, v)}      & read $\mu(u,v)$ \\
\code{hasEdge(G, u, v)}           & whether $\mu(u,v) > 0$ \\
\code{degree(G, v)}               & total degree of $v$ (in- plus out-degree if directed) \\
\code{edgeCount(G)}               & number of edges or arcs, counted with multiplicity \\
\code{edges(G)}, \code{vertices(G)} & iterate the present edges, and the vertex set \\
\code{copy(G)}                    & an independent duplicate \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{The routine operations on the graph model. Each is a one-line wrapper that reads or writes the matrix $\mu$ while keeping an undirected graph symmetric and a simple graph binary. They are bookkeeping, and the chapter's attention belongs to the routines that follow.}
\label{tab:graph-ops}
\end{table}

Every write to $\mu$ passes through one helper that mirrors the entry when the
model is undirected. Thus symmetry is maintained in one place rather than left
as an invariant for callers.
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{J6. Simulated annealing versus tabu benchmark}{J6}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former subsection and table}{after the rediscovery discussion}{The reference-machine timing comparison is implementation evidence, not a mathematical result; the live text retains which search is used and why.}
\subsection{Simulated annealing versus tabu search}

The annealer is not the only way to walk this landscape. A natural alternative, suggested by Jan Goedgebeur, is \emph{tabu search}, which keeps the same energy and adjacency-toggle neighbourhood but replaces the cooling schedule with a deterministic best-improving step. At each step it takes the single-edge move that lowers the energy most, then forbids the pair it just changed for a fixed number of steps, the \emph{tabu tenure}, so the walk cannot immediately undo its last move and stall in a two-step cycle, and when no improving move is left it perturbs the incumbent and carries on. \Cref{tab:sa-vs-tabu} runs the two head to head at an equal budget of six seconds each. On the simple undirected problem both reach the optimum at once. On the two directed problems tabu reaches the target within the budget while the annealer plateaus below it, at sixteen arcs against eighteen and at twenty against the full twenty-four-arc double star, both at $n = 7$. The cause is the one already met at the $n = 8$, $m = 2$ hub stall above: the annealer cools onto whatever dense shape it first reaches and then cannot dismantle it within the budget, whereas tabu's best-improving step follows the steepest density gradient and the tabu list stops it sliding back.

\begin{table}[ht]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{lllrrrcc}
        \toprule
         & & & & \multicolumn{2}{c}{best in $6$\,s} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{time to optimum} \\
        \cmidrule(lr){5-6}\cmidrule(lr){7-8}
        Variant & $n$ & $m$ & optimum & SA & tabu & SA & tabu \\
        \midrule
        simple undirected   & 7 & 3 & $9$  & $9$  & $9$  & $1.3$\,s & $0.1$\,s \\
        simple directed     & 7 & 3 & $18$ & $16$ & $18$ & not reached & $2.6$\,s \\
        directed multigraph & 7 & 3 & $24$ & $20$ & $24$ & not reached & $2.5$\,s \\
        \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{SA and tabu search at equal six-second budgets for $m=3$. Tabu reaches all three targets; SA reaches the undirected optimum but plateaus below both directed targets. Times are reference-machine wall clock.}
    \label{tab:sa-vs-tabu}
\end{table}

Each tabu step scans the whole neighbourhood, so it is far more expensive than the annealer's single random proposal, and takes many fewer but better-aimed steps in the same wall-clock, a cost the equal-time comparison already absorbs. Tabu search is therefore the program's default for solving the problems, reaching every value this thesis reports, the rediscovery checks and the conjectural lower bounds alike, and reaching the harder directed optima past the exhausted range where the annealer plateaus. Simulated annealing is kept for the self-check and verification, where the small values fall at once, the lighter per-step cost is all that is needed, and its independent restarts parallelise trivially across cores.
\end{offcut}

\begin{offcut}{J7. Edge sensitivity section}{J7}{chapters/ch2\_machine.tex, former Section 2.6}{after the heuristic-search restarts paragraph}{$\sigma(e)$ is a removal-proposal bias used only inside the annealer, which \Cref{ch:certify} already names and describes without it, and tabu, the engine every reported value in this thesis comes from, never uses it. The author judged the colouring-a-witness diagnostic use not reason enough to keep the section. Author instruction, 27 August 2026.}
\section{Edge sensitivity}

For an edge $e$, define its \emph{sensitivity}
\[
    \sigma(e) = \lambda^{\max}(G) - \lambda^{\max}(G - e),
\]
the amount by which deleting $e$ relaxes the binding connectivity. A load-bearing edge sits on a tightest cut and has $\sigma(e) > 0$: removing it would drop $\lambda^{\max}$ and so loosen the very constraint that limits how many edges the graph may hold. A free edge has $\sigma(e) = 0$, so removing it eases nothing, and it can usually be put to better use elsewhere. The program measures $\sigma$ by deleting $e$ and letting the checker remeasure against a baseline $\lambda^{\max}(G)$ shared by every edge, so a whole graph's sensitivity map costs one flow per edge rather than one per edge plus a repeated baseline.

Sensitivity plays no part in the energy $\Phi$: it never scores a candidate graph, only chooses which edge a removal move tries next, and only inside the annealer. Rather than choosing a present edge uniformly, the annealer draws its removal target with weight $\exp(-\sigma(e)/T)$, so a load-bearing edge is disfavoured, and the disfavouring sharpens as $T$ cools towards zero. Building the map costs one flow per edge, so it is cached and refreshed only once every \code{bias\_refresh} steps rather than before every proposal, at the price of acting on a map that is briefly out of date. Tabu search carries no such bias and needs none: at every step it already scores every move in its whole neighbourhood exactly before taking the best one, so there is nothing left for a cheap hint to buy. Since every discovery value this thesis reports comes from tabu, sensitivity shapes none of them. It remains in the program as the annealer's own device and as part of the independent cross-check the annealer provides.

Colouring a graph's arcs by $\sigma$ reads a finished witness at a glance regardless of which engine produced it. \Cref{fig:sensitivity} shows all four sensitivity levels on one directed multigraph: the darkest arcs carry the most flow and lower $\lambda^{\max}$ the most when removed, while the cool arc contributes nothing. It turns a bare edge count into something a reader can check the shape of.

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{figures/sensitivity_mixed.png}
    \caption{Arc sensitivity in a directed multigraph with $\lambda^{\max}=9$, each parallel copy drawn as its own arc so multiplicity is read by counting rather than from a label. Equal multiplicities can have different $\sigma$: the downstream bottleneck makes $s\to a$ cooler than $s\to b$, while arcs into the dead end $d$ have $\sigma=0$.}
    \label{fig:sensitivity}
\end{figure}
\end{offcut}
